## Funding proposal package for FP121

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "REDD+ Results-based payments in Paraguay for the period 2019-2023";

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;

d) Secretary's assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Board of Directors' accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

g) Gender documentation.

# Funding Proposal

## REDD-plus results based payments

### Version 1.0

Accredited entities are expected to develop a funding proposal in close consultation with the relevant national designated authority and REDD-plus entity/focal point, in response to the request for proposals for the Pilot Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (Document GCF/8.19). The funding proposal will follow the terms of reference that Board decision and will be assessed per Stage 2 (Sections 2 – 5) of the scorecard annexed to the same Board decision.

**National Designated Authority:** Technical Secretariat for Economic and Social Development Planning (STP)

**REDD-plus entity/focal point:** Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADES)

E.1.1. UNFCCC Technical Assessment and Analysis process

*Consistency of the FREL/FRL: Paraguay has provided additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the consistency of the FREL/FRL with the GHG Inventory.*

Paraguay submitted its first Forest Reference Emission Levels (FREL) on a voluntary basis on 4th January 2016, in accordance with Decision 12/C.P. Technical evaluation of the FREL took place in March 2016, resulting in an assessment report being published by FAO in May 2016. The assessment report provides the necessary clarifications and information to improve the transparency and consistency of the FREL.

As a result, Paraguay submitted a modified version of the FREL that considered the technical inputs provided by the Technical Assessment Team (TAT).

The FREL (modified version of the FREL, Technical Assessment (TA) Conclusion) states as follows:

*The information used by Paraguay for the construction of the FREL for deforestation is overall transparent and complete and is in overall accordance with the guidelines for the FREL information submission (as contained in Decision 12/C.P.)*

Consistency of the FREL/FRL with the GHG Inventory

Paraguay has also provided additional information to support the National Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Inventory for the Land Use, Land Use Change and Forestry (LULUCF) sector. The Ministry of Environment and Forests (MINEFOR), in collaboration with the National Forest Institute (INIFOR) and the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS), have coordinated activities within the national forest monitoring system (NFMS). These activities include the collection, processing and validation of data for the construction of the GHG Inventory and the construction of the FREL, as well as for other national reports. For this reason, the information used by Paraguay to construct both documents is consistent.

The methods used in the two reports have been similar, only differing in some specific aspects (included gases and carbon pools) due to the different available information at the time each document was developed.

During the Technical Assessment the following inconsistencies were identified:

Forest Definition

As part of the REDD+ readiness phase Paraguay has facilitated discussion to agree on the national definition of forest. The agreed definition is that a forest is defined as an area of land with trees forming a continuous ecosystem with biodiversity, intervened or not, regenerated and/or restored by natural succession or by forest management activities, which provides timber products, non-timber forest products (NTFPs), environmental services, with a minimum area of 1 hectare (ha), with a tree height equal to or greater than 3 meters (m) for the eastern region and 2.5 m in the western region. The definition also includes areas where the forest cover in its natural status of 50% of the western region and 30% for the eastern region. It also includes native paludiculture systems, with a minimum area of 1 ha, with a tree height equal to or greater than 2 m, and an area width of 60 m wide and equal or greater than 1 ha are also include as forest. This forest definition excludes urban areas, agricultural land, aquaculture plantations, agroforestry systems and silvopastoral systems, where the main purpose is agriculture.

This definition is in accordance with the one used for the Greenhouse Gas Emissions Report of Paraguay (2015) and the Forest Reference Emission Levels (FREL) Technical Assessment Report (BUR 2015) and later in the 7th National Communication (2017) and 8th BUR (2018), in compliance with Decision 12/C.P.

During the FREL TA, an inconsistency was identified in the definition used by the Forest Resource Assessment (FRA) 2010. The document, published by FAO in 2015 and submitted by MINA for prior to the FREL TA, defines forest as an area of land with trees forming a continuous ecosystem with biodiversity, intervened or not, regenerated and/or restored by natural succession or by forest management activities, which provides timber products, non-timber forest products (NTFPs), environmental services. The definitions were also used within the framework of Law 2524 (Zero Deforestation Law for Paraguay’s Eastern Region) and Law 1830 (Forest Code).

Paraguay's response has been to clarify that the definition adopted for the FREL is the most up-to-date and that it results from an inter-institutional agreement reached as part of the country's REDD+ readiness

**Framework:** There is an agreement in place between MADES and INFONA to maintain the use of this definition for future reporting, as was the case in the 5th BUR National GHG Inventory. The same applies with the REDD+ Technical Annex. Additionally, future reports under the UNFCCC and the upcoming FRA will be based on this methodology.

In the consultation process with the AT, additional clarifications were made with regard to the technical soundness of the methodology and remote sensing images (median spatial resolution, 30 x 30 m) used for the forest cover change analysis. The same applies in the case of the REDD+ Technical Annex.

As a result, Paraguay has included these technical limitations as areas for future improvement. MADES and INFONA are currently analyzing satellite imagery from the Sentinel 2 series (European Space Agency), which have a higher spatial resolution than those used to generate the FREL. These images will be evaluated and validated to ensure the integration or comparability of the results.

**Gases and carbon pools included:**

The FREL includes information on GHG emissions from the National Forest Inventory (NFI) for the Bosque Submedio del Cerrodo (BSCD) and for the Bosque Sub-Humedo Humedalde del Rio Paraguay (BSHPR). These studies were completed before the FREL, but the data was not available at the time of the BUR. The GHG inventory for the LULUCF sector of Paraguay's 1st BUR (2015) had to be completed before the FREL, and the results are included in the FREL.

Default values have a high associated uncertainty level**, as they were estimated at a regional scale.

Another difference is that Paraguay’s LULUCF NGH Global GHG Inventory (1st BUR) considered the variation of carbon dioxide (CO2), nitrous oxide (N2O), and methane (CH4). For the FREL, only CO2 emissions from forest land conversion (native forests) were considered. The default values for N2O and CH4 are not available at a national scale.

Paraguay has also clarified that there are differences in terms of carbon pools and reported gases. For the FREL, only Above and Below Ground (AGB), BGB living biomass pools and biomass of undervegetation and LCO emissions were considered.

Paraguay’s FREL mentions that the LULUCF NGH Global GHG Inventory only reports on CO2 (Carbon Dioxide) and N2O (Nitrous Oxide Diode) emissions. The reason for this difference is availability for the FREL's entire reporting period.

In response to this observation from the TA, Paraguay explained that the omission in the FREL is due to the fact that according to the National GHG Inventory (LULUCF) for the reporting year 2015, N2O emissions were 8.96 Gt CO2-eq and CH4 was 3.75 Gt CO2-eq. The omission of these gases in the FREL is justified because they represent less than 10% of the emission in relation to CO2 equivalents; therefore, it is not considered a significant omission.

The TA found that the Nitrous Oxide exclusion is justified based on the information provided. Also, it considers that the treatment of gases other than CO2 should be included as areas for future improvement given the technical soundness and comparability with the REDD+ Technical Annex.

Paraguay's response confirms the need to include these improvements in the country’s future estimates. During the FREL, considerations were made regarding the inclusion of N2O and CH4 emissions from forest land conversion activity (e.g., burning). Paraguay's response has acknowledged the need to make improvements for example, by including N2O and CH4 emissions from wildfires for the period 2005-2013, as well as data on post-land-use land use, to include in future reports.

**A further difference mentioned in the FREL is the inclusion of Above Ground Biomass, Below Ground Biomass and Undervegetation biomass pools. These are not included in the LULUCF NGH Global GHG Inventory (1st BUR). The material (dead wood, litter) and carbon found in soil (whether mineral or organic) were not considered. However, in relation to the FREL, these emissions are considered as part of the land use change category (land conversion) (mineral soils), as the contribution to CO2 emissions was considered significant (39.1%) for the reported year.

Paraguay justified its decision to exclude this carbon pool from the FREL because the available information is only for forest cover, of which, according to the available information available on the volume of emissions in the transfer/changes in land use after the native forest conversion.

The country has also mentioned that there are not enough studies or scientific research in the country that could provide reliable data on "organic pools," as reported in the Third National Communication (2011).

During the assessment of the FREL's carbon pools and gases, the evaluating team considered that the omissions of pools and gases are probably conservative in this context.

Improvement Plan

In response to these observations, Paraguay is making continuous efforts to enhance the information and data in accordance with the recommendations.

In this regard, after the conclusion of the FREL, the National Forestry Institute continued with the collection and analysis of forest inventory data. The final results for the forest strata in Paraguay, including data on dead wood, leaves and mineral soils:

Part of these efforts focus on the reanalysis of some of the forest strata in 2018 and 2019, as well as to plan the collection of new samples in the year 2020. In addition, it is planned that the measurement of new sample units in a proportion of 25% in 2020 and 2021, and an additional 25% in 2022.

With regard to the forest cover dynamics, there are plans to obtain better data on post-deforestation land use and to update the FREL.

The country has also made progress to improve its national capacities to implement the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for the AFOLU sector in its country reports. These efforts are aimed mainly at helping the country to maintain and improve the quality of its forest inventory data, as well as to ensure that it is updated regularly and up-to-date.

The data source of the FREL/FRLR: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information provided by the National Forestry Institute in relation to the data used for the construction of the FREL/FRLR, specifying whether the FREL/FRLR is based on historical data and is equal to or below the emission factor values.

Developed in 2016, based on historical gross deforestation data. Paraguay's Forest Reference Emissions Level represents estimated emissions of 58,763,378.14 tons of CO₂ equivalent per year, totaling an average of 942.17 tons of CO₂ equivalents per annum for the period of analysis (15 years from 2005 to 2015).

This calculation was obtained by combining activity data (gross deforestation for native forest strata) with their respective emission factors for each native forest stratum.

Activity Data

The development of Paraguay's FREL is based on historical data of forest cover changes for a 15-year period (2000-2014). The years selected were: 2000, 2005, 2010 and 2015.

The changes that occurred between 2002 and 2015 are determined by the analysis of four periods: 2000-2005, 2005-2011, 2011-2013, and 2013-2015. The years were selected based on the time and resources available to support the development of the FREL. Also, the selected period was aimed at generating data for the country's LULUCF National Inventory Report (NIR) in accordance with the requirements established by the Kyoto Protocol and reported according to the country's commitments, among them the Forest Resources Assessment (FRA) report.

To make this possible, a forest cover map of Paraguay for 2015 was generated. This forest cover map of the year 2011 was generated; from a forest cover map of the Bosque Palmer for the same year was generated. These maps were obtained by combining the data provided by the National Forestry Institute and the data of the National Forest Inventory, which are: (i) Bosque Húmedo de la Región Oriental (BROH); (ii) Bosque Subhúmedo de la Región Oriental; (iii) Bosque Seco Tropical Humedo montano; (iv) Bosque Paraguarí (Bosque seco chaqueño (BSC)); y (v) Bosque de Palmer (BP).

This map for the year 2011 combines the native forest and the Bosque Palmer forest maps and is the basis for later forest cover change analysis.

### Figure 1: Paraguay's Forest Cover Map by Strata and Location

**Source:** Paraguay’s Forest Reference Emissions Level 2016.

The analysis of the forest cover change was carried out in a two-step process, in which three types of cover were identified for each stratum during the period analyzed. First, the analysis included deforestation from 2011 backwards for the periods 2000-2005 and 2005-2011. Then, the analysis was performed on a yearly basis.

During the TA Assessment Team noted that the annualized average (2000-2005 and 2005-2011; 5 and 6 years respectively) may overestimate or underestimate the values for a particular year.

A further issue was raised during the TA regarding the Bosque Palmer, which does not have a spatially explicit change map. The Bosque Palmer is located in the South-Western part of Paraguay (SWPAR). Due to the Bosque Palmer's characteristics, which has a highly variable tree density and canopy cover, it is difficult to accurately determine its extent.

In response, Paraguay stated that these considerations were considered; but due to timing, capabilities, and available resources they were included as areas for future improvements. Currently the country already possesses a change map of Bosque Palmer at the national level on an annual basis, and it is expected to have a spatially explicit change map for Bosque Palmer by 2019.

#### Methodology for cartographic products

The methodology used was based on the Remote Monitoring System (RMS) to generate cartographic products was developed and gradually improved to achieve replicability, comparability and transparency and to ensure consistency in the results. The process involved several steps: first, a supervised classification processes to generate the first land cover/use maps; then, an object-based image analysis approach using the software ENVI 4.7 to generate the second land cover/use maps (for different analysis periods from 2013-2015); and finally, a Google Earth Engine was used to generate forest cover and land-use change maps (for different analysis periods from 2013-2015). The data processing is carried out on the server of the ENVI software, which means that all the processing and data storage are carried out on a server that hosts the user's information (Phangas Forest Reserve Emission Level).

Finally, the forest cover change maps generated (for the four periods) were evaluated for the assessment of uncertainties and errors associated with the methodology. This thematic accuracy assessment, whose methodology is described in detail in Annex 1, was carried out by an independent team of experts using supervised classification processes to generate the first land cover/use maps; then, an object-based image analysis approach using the software ENVI 4.7 to generate the second land cover/use maps (for different analysis periods from 2013-2015); and finally, a Google Earth Engine was used to generate forest cover and land-use change maps (for different analysis periods from 2013-2015). The accuracy of these thematic assessments is estimated by an independent mapping team composed of remote sensing technicians from the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MARN) and the National Forest Service (SERNAT), which are responsible for generating the maps and the team evaluating them guaranteeing objectivity in determining the reliability of their products.

### Emission Factor

To estimate the emission factor (EF), Paraguay used the IPCC good practice guidelines (2003) approach (Tier 1 and Tier 2). The Tier 2 is based on the use of country-specific data.

The specific data generated for this country was used as part of the National Forest Inventory (NFI) that carried out the forest inventory in Paraguay. The NFI was conducted using a stratified random sampling design. For the identification of the total biomass, allometric equations were derived locally for the strata of Bosque Húmedo del Río Paraguay (BHP), Bosque Seco del Chaco (BSC), Bosque Seco de Montaña (BSM), and Bosque Subhúmedo Humidificado del Río Paraguay (SHH). These equations were used to estimate the biomass of the forest strata. The BHP was further divided into three sub-strata: bottom ground, lower bottom ground, and upper bottom ground.

Source: Paraguay's Forest Reference Emissions Level 2016.


The Bosque Palmer and the Bosque Subhúmedo Humidificado del Río Paraguay strata presented a difficulty in association with the BHP due to the lack of weighty specific data for all periods of change (Bosque Palmer is included within the BSHPR). See previous section for activity data as this point. In the original version of PREL, the BHP was considered as a single stratum and not differentiated including the BP, but without any discrimination.

The factors used in the calculation are an observation on this point, considering that this calculation would overestimate the emission factor because the BSHPR has an emission factor higher than the BP.


In response to this, Paraguay applied a weighted average by area (using as a reference the BP relative area in relation to the total forest area of the country). The result was a weighted average value for the emission factor. This weighted average value of the emission factor allows the country to obtain a more conservative value regarding CO2 emissions from deforestation and degradation, which is consistent with the REDD+ methodology. Paraguay must make improvements in the differentiation of spatially explicit changes for this strata. This was identified as an area of improvement by PREL during the validation process. Paraguay expects to have the Bosque Palmer Forest Cover Change Maps (for different periods) by 2019.

The data compilation made by the National Forest Inventory is based on the methodology described in the National Forest Inventory (NFI) and the PREL (2017) guidelines. The NFI uses a stratified random sampling design, Processing and Analysis Methodology generated within the framework of Paraguay's UN-REDD National Programme.

The AT assessed the use of Allometric Equations, and recommended comparing the results obtained from the application of allometric equations and those obtained from the calculation of geometric volume, including expansion factors for each species. It was concluded that the use of a single equation to estimate biomass by this method may not adequately represent each strain due to the high diversity of species found in them.

The present study is part of an ongoing project aimed at developing new allometric equations for future implementation since there was insufficient time and resources for such a study at that moment. Currently, INFOFA's National Forest Inventory (NFI) uses the same allometric equations as those used by the country to compare the calculations for both methods and analyzing the technical and economic feasibility of experiments related to studies related to new Allometric Equations for Paraguay's native forest status.

The present study was aimed at developing new allometric equations for estimating biomass obtained for the country's different forest types. To calculate this, the allometric equations generated at forest strain level were used as a reference in order to obtain the average values of each species and then apply them to the total volume. Underground biomass was obtained through the subtraction of the result from total live biomass.

The estimation of carbon and CO<sub>2</sub> equivalent content from tree biomass was made by applying the default average factor from the 2006 IPCC guidelines (value of 0.47, average range 0.44 to 0.49) for subtropical forests (over the estimated live biomass of 500 m<sup>3</sup>).

For the understorey vegetation, the carbon fraction was calculated based on the NFI field samples which were analyzed in laboratory using the dry biomass combustion method, at a temperature of approximately 900°C. Finally, for the emission factors calculation it was assumed that the biomass right after deforestation is equal to zero because it corresponds to the assimilated carbon stored in-advance.

For the construction of the Forest Reference Emissions Level, Paraguay has followed the guidelines of UNFCCC in the Decision 12/CP.17 for the presentation of FREL/RFL, in relation to:

- a) Report the information used for the FRE/LRF construction.
- b) Ensure consistency, completeness and accuracy in the methodological information used for the FRE/LRF construction process.

Paraguay has made a significant effort to present its first FREL and has also committed to strengthening its capacity to construct future FRELs. The TA Report identified several issues that were material regarding the implementation of a step-wise approach. However, some issues have been identified which are included in a revised version of the FREL submitted.

Also, the issues observed by the TA did not identify significant issues for the FRE/LRF estimation.

Finally, according to the Technical Assessment, the information used by Paraguay for the construction of the FRE/LRF for deforestation is in overall accordance with the guidelines for the submission of information on FRE/LRF as contained in Decision 12/CP.17.

#### (c) FRE/LRF transparency: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the transparency of the FREL/RFL and whether the raised issues were material or not material to transparency, as per Decision 12/CMP.17.

The exchange between the FRE/LAT and the Government of Paraguay allowed the identification of additional relevant information that contributes to the improvement and transparency of Paraguay's FREL.

The original version of the FREL submitted included four annexes, which were further enhanced with the submission of a revised version. The six annexes in the modified submission contain the following information:

1. A methodological description of the elaboration of the 2011 forest cover map, the 2011 Bosque Palmer map and the 2011 Forest Inventory.
2. The methodology used to assess the uncertainty and accuracy associated with land-use change maps.
3. Data processing methods for the National Forest Inventory.
4. Presentation of the results from the analysis of the reference period for the National Forest Inventory.
5. Reference information from other data sources on Bosque Sub Humedo Inundable del Rio Paraguay and its surroundings.

The AT considered that transparency and completeness improved significantly with the submission of the modified FRE/LRF and commended Paraguay's efforts.

**vii) FRE/LRL accuracy**: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the accuracy of the FRE/LRL, and whether significant issues exist with respect to under- or over-estimates of emissions and/or removals during the reference period. The Technical Assessment team acknowledges that data is not material to the accuracy of the FRE/LRL, and that could be resolved due to date and data restrictions.

The accuracy of the Reference Level can be determined based on the assessments made for both activity data and emission factors.

During the FREL construction, the lack of spatially explicit maps for the Bosque Palmer has been identified as one of the issues to be addressed, since it is currently included within the Bosque Subandino Inmutable (the FREL Palmer cover change map). Paraguay has been to carry a weighted average calculation considering the area of each of its strata.

The AT considers this to be an interim solution. However, they stated that palm areas should be treated as an independent activity and should be assessed with respect to their own emission factors. This will improve the accuracy of the estimates and avoid potential over estimation of emissions.

As mentioned previously, Paraguay has taken into consideration the observations of the AT, and adopted the proposed methodology for the calculation of the reference level. The impact on the FREL Palmer cover change map for different periods of analysis are expected to be completed by 2019. This means that there will be no issues in future to improve the FRE/LRL calculations and to report results.

**Accuracy of Activity Data**

Paraguay has also provided information on the accuracy of the two land-use change maps for the periods 2000-2005,2011 and 2013-2015, including a detailed description of the methodology. For uncertainty estimation, they have considered uncertainties.

**Accuracy of Emission Factors**

The FREL contains information on the assessment of the associated uncertainty of carbon content in each of the native forest strata for above-ground biomass, below-ground biomass and undervegetation biomass pools.

The AT comments Paraguay for the development and provision of specific information on uncertainty, accuracy and ongoing efforts to increase the accuracy of emissions in future releases.

Paraguay has also addressed issues into account and has incorporated them as part of future improvement plans under the step-wise approach.

The FREL also mentions that the activity of "reducing emissions from the degradation of natural forest" is in the discussion and analysis stage, so it was not considered appropriate to include information on this topic at this stage.

Paraguay is currently finalizing the first studies to agree on a national definition for degradation and to determine the methods for monitoring and reporting on deforestation and forest degradation emission factors.

The National Forest Inventory is one of many sources of information for assessing forest degradation. Within the framework of the inventory, there are plans to carry out remeasurement of the five different strata and inclusion of new areas in the next cycle.

### (a) Significant points: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the inclusion of the most significant pools. If applicable, justify why these pools should be included or excluded from the calculation of deforestation emissions or underestimate emissions in addition. Provide a plan to include all significant pools in future FRELs.

Paraguay has prioritized above-ground biomass, below-ground biomass and understory vegetation biomass.

After evaluating the pools and gases included in the FREL, the TA observed that the emissions of pools and gases are probably conservative. Additionally, it identifies the following areas for future technical improvement:

- Collection of data on the dynamics of carbon content in deadwood, litter and mineral soils after forest conversion.
- Collection of the necessary information to estimate emissions from deforested organic soils to justify their inclusion as a pool.
- Treatment of other emissions of other gases (other than CO2) and emissions from slash and burn conversion.

### Action Plan for future technical improvements

Since 2018 the NIFIS is endorsed by Law No. 6259 to provide official information on the state of national forest cover at a resolution of 30 meters.

The structure of coordination between MADES and INFONA is currently in the process of being regulated by an Official Decree.

The Government has proposed a flow chart for the NIFIS (see BUR and BUR Technical Annex, page 271) that can be used as a reference to guide the implementation of the FREL.

INFONA implements the National Forest Inventory (NFI) with the cooperation of MADES, for the generation of Emission Factors and the Satellite Land Monitoring System that generates the National Forest Cover Map and the National Forest Change Map.

MADES develops the Thematic Assessment of the National Forest Cover Map and Land-Use/Land-Cover Map, which is used as a reference to generate the NFI data for the LULUCF sector.

INFONA receives and incorporates the results from the Thematic Accuracy Assessment.

IV. MADES approves and incorporates the results in the National Communications. National GHG inventories are prepared by INFONA and approved by MADES.

The coordination between the institutions has already started to contribute to the areas identified for future technical improvements, which include:

- Improving the capacity of the estimation of uncertainties both for activity data and for emission factors of the current inventory and of the land and forest.
- Improving the technical capacity of officials, both in INFONA and MADES, in order to ensure the sustainability of the work.

### (ii) IPCC guidance for FRE/LRL: Please include if whether the construction of the FRE/LRL (lists, methodology, etc.) is in accordance with the latest IPCC Good Practice Guidance.

The FRE/LRL construction was guided by the IPCC Good Practice Guidance on Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry (LULUCF) sector.

### For the estimation of activity data, a methodology corresponding to a level 3 of completely described in the 2003 IPCC Good Practice Guidance document was used. The methodology uses geographically explicit land use and land cover information.

For the emission factors' estimation, Paraguay has implemented the National Forest Inventory within the framework of the country's REDD+ efforts. This implies that the country has taken into consideration the IPCC guidelines for LULUCF sector.

The analyses performed to obtain the emission factors for both FREL and BUR’s National GHG Inventory for the LULUCF sector (2015) have also followed the 2003 GPGs guidelines.

Additionally, for the calculation of 2006 GLR default values have been used for the AFOLU Sector. For example, the dry matter ratio of 0.47 was used in the AFOLU sector.

The data, methodologies and estimates that Paraguay are developing are aimed at enabling the country to use the most up-to-date IPCC guidance in the future.

Please indicate whether the country has provided information on aggregated uncertainties of the activity data and emissions factors used in the calculation of its national forest carbon stock. If so, please indicate the percentage of aggregate uncertainties and provide information on assumptions and sources. If not, please describe how these uncertainties were calculated.

The construction of the FREL has made significant efforts to reduce the uncertainties associated with the activity data and emission factors used.

## Activity Data

This calculation assesses the reliability of the Land Use Change Map for the period 2005-2009-2011 showing a total of 86.1% of overall accuracy considering the surface of each forest type per stratum. For the Land Use Change Map, the forest types were grouped into three categories: native forest (strata), secondary forest (strata) and other forest types (strata). The area of each forest type is expressed in hectares.

The calculations to determine the accuracy of both maps were performed using "stratified random sampling". This means that the sample was stratified according to different stratum (other, native forest, secondary forest) homogeneity with respect to some characteristic. The purpose of this type of sampling is to ensure that the stratification is representative and that the statistical analysis works independently, although a simple random sampling can be applied to each of them separately.

## Emission Factor

For the assessment of the uncertainty of the Emission Factor, the methodologies and procedures identified in the data processing document of the National Forest Inventory was used. This was done for each of the native forest strata (stratum) and other forest types (stratum). The results are expressed as percentages of ground and underlying vegetation biomass carbon. The values obtained are presented in percentage in the following table.

The National Forest Inventory, which provides the data for the estimation of emission factors, was designed and implemented in a way that minimizes the occurrence of errors or biases in order to achieve high statistical, economic and time-efficiency. The field data collection was carried out in 2014 and 2015 and includes a methodology for the calculation of uncertainty.

Regarding aggregated uncertainties, the FREL did not make this estimate due to limitations in relation to activity data (Bosque Palmer without spatially explicit cover for all periods) and the use of preliminary NFI results. Since it is planned to use the new map, it will be possible to calculate the uncertainty at the time of calculation of NFI results. It was planned to obtain these data and complement them with new map results.

The following formula was used to calculate the emission reductions from gross deforestation:

REDD+ Reductions (2015-2016-2017): mCO₂eq/ year = (FREL × (period 2000 - 2015, tCO₂eq/year)) / (Gross emissions from deforestation for the period 2015-2016-2017, tCO₂eq/year)

Paraguay developed a methodology during the construction of the FREL that was consistently followed throughout the analysis of historical emissions (FREL) as well as for the analyzing emission results during the reporting period.

## Consistency between the FREL 2016 and REDD+ results

- The LULUCF experts that carried out the technical assessment noted that Paraguay ensured overall consistency between its FREL and its estimation of the results by:
    - Using the same methodology and data to generate activity data (AD) and using the same forest inventory.
    - Using consistent methodologies and data to generate Emission Factors (EFs), using the same carbon stock for each disaggregated forest strata.
- The results reported include only emissions from gross deforestation (REDD+ activity).
- Only Carbon Dioxide (CO2) emissions from the pools mentioned above are included.
- Covering the entire national territory.

## Consistency between the REDD+ results estimates and the BUR

The results reported in the Technical Annex have identified the same inconsistencies that were reported in the FREL and its corresponding Technical Assessment during the 1st BUR (2015) for Paraguay's development of the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS). These consistencies are:
- The use of different methodologies and data to generate activity data (AD) and using the same forest inventory.
- The use of consistent methodologies and data to generate Emission Factors (EFs), using the same carbon stock for each disaggregated forest strata.

MAIDES, in confirmation with INFONAT, as the institution responsible for the NFMS, is generating the necessary information to be able to apply the methodology and data used by Paraguay during revision and/or adjustment of the FREL. This will allow inconsistencies to be eliminated in the National GHG Inventory in the LULUCF sector of the BUR.

## Forest definition

Paraguay used the same forest definition for estimating the REDD+ results (2015-2017) and the FREL. This definition differs from the one used for the 2015 Global Forest Resources Assessment (UN FAO) and the Kyoto Protocol under the clean development mechanism. The forest definition has different thresholds to


---
**Page 23**

GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE [20 of 83])

Maximum forest cover and tree height based on the ecological conditions in the regions (minimum forest cover of 30% and a minimum average tree height of 5 m for the western region; 40% and 10 m for the eastern region). However, the remote sensing analysis using medium-resolution satellite images are effective for 20-40% of minimum forest cover. The Assessment Team noted that this is an area for future technical assessment.

Paraguay has agreed with implementing the use of satellite imagery like the sensor Sentinel could be used to monitor the forest in the 30-year period since the last INFOR.

Availability of updated data:

Paraguay, through the National Forestry Institute, with support from the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MIDES), is currently updating the FREL (data collection and processing and generation of results) of the National Forest Inventory after the construction of the 2016 FREL and its corresponding INFOR.

This process has generated new data that are considered final results, which will be used to improve the emission factors used in future FRELs.

However, to maintain consistency and comparability of 2015-2017 results with the 2016 FREL, the same emission factors were applied for the 2015-2017 period.

(II) Transparency of the data: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Analysis Report on the transparency of the data and information related to the results of the analysis of the REDD+ Annexes (the electronic spreadsheet). An understanding of the UNFCCC guidance on results reporting has been included. Also, information on significant issues raised in the Technical Analysis Report is provided.

The data used for the analysis of the results are available on the website: http://www.infor.gov.py/infor.php/604

More specifically, the following information and data related to the generation of the results are available:

- Map of Forest Cover change 2015-2016.
- Map of Forest Cover change 2016-2017.
- Map of Forest Cover change 2015-2017.
- Map Evaluation Thematic ArcGIS 2015-2017.
- The thematic evaluation report (the electronic spreadsheet) and the REDD+ TA (electronic spreadsheet).
- Calculation of Results of the REDD+ Technical Annexes (electronic spreadsheet).

On the same website, the data and information regarding the process of building the FREL are also available, and they can be found at: http://www.infor.gov.py/infor.php/591

Additionally, during the Technical Analysis of the results by the ICA, other information has been made available to the evaluators, such as: an electronic spreadsheet of the thematic evaluation containing error matrices, references to the INFOR database and the process of evaluation of the data and other data requested as object-based classification segments (shapesfiles).

To improve the transparency of the results, INFOR is in the process of adding the geographic data of the results for each year at: http://www.infor.gov.py/infor.php/591

Additionally, the methodology was applied to define the activity data analyzing the changes in the forest cover for each year of the analysis period (2015-2016 and 2016-2017). Unlike the FREL that carried out the analyses for periods of five years (2003-2008, 2004-2009, 2005-2010, 2006-2011, 2007-2012 and 2008-2013), the INFOR carried out the analyses for periods of two years (2015-2016 and 2016-2017).

One of the issues observed by the evaluation team was the difference between the deforested area in the 2015-2016 period and the 2016-2017 period. The possible causes of the difference have been discussed.


---
**Page 24**
---

Paraguay’s response was that it has not been possible to identify with certainty the cause of this trend. However, the assessment team noted that expectations generated by a government study on deforestation increase. In fact, in September 2017, the former administration enacted Decree 775/2017 under the motivation that the country was facing an emergency situation due to deforestation.

Shortly after the new government came into office, the new authorities of Paraguay decreed Decree 775/2017, ending the opportunity created to allow deforestation which was in clear disagreement with the environmental objectives and commitments made by Paraguay under the Paris Agreement. The decree entered into force on October 31, 2017.

Preliminary data show that the level of deforestation had decreased, and is closer to the average of the 2015-2016 period (see Figure 4).

Another consideration highlighted by the assessment team was the number of months of effective analysis during the results period. Paraguay clarified that, due to time and administrative constraints during the preparation phase of the REDD+ Technical Annex, the period from January 2017 to December 2017 corresponds to approximately 9 months, while the period of 2016-2017 corresponds to approximately 13 months. This means a total of 21 months of analysis for the period 2015-2017.

Furthermore, Paraguay clarified that deforestation that was not detected or counted in the first period (2015-2016) is accounted in the second period (2016-2017), and those that are not counted in the second period will be accounted for in subsequent analysis periods.

### Aggregated uncertainties

One of the limitations discussed during the technical analysis process was that Paraguay’s REDD+ Technical Annex does not include an estimate of the emission propagation.

More specifically, the evaluators consulted if the calculation had been made and asked Paraguay to perform the analysis of global uncertainties for the emissions factor by the activity date for the period of results as well as for the construction of the 2015-2017 FRL. Paraguay responded that it was not possible.

Paraguay responded to the request during the assessment process resulting in the following explanation provided by the LULUCF experts:

The LULUCF experts also noted that the technical annex does not include aggregated uncertainties of emissions over time, and thus, they cannot assign global uncertainties for the emissions factor. To respond, Paraguay calculated the aggregated uncertainty, propagating the error of AD to EFs at 23 per cent for the emission estimation period (2015-2017). The LULUCF experts commend Paraguay for calculating its aggregated uncertainty.

If not, provide a plan on how to address and overcome issues raised in the Technical Annex of the BUR.

The data used by Paraguay are consistent with the data and methodologies used over time. The data can be resolved due to time and date restrictions.

Provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the technical annex (i.e., whether data and methodologies were applied consistently over the results time series). If not, provide a plan on how to address and overcome issues raised in the Technical Annex of the BUR. The data used by Paraguay are consistent with the consistency of the data and results that could be resolved due to time and date restrictions.

---
**Page 25**
---

One of the limitations of the activity data is that the country does not have a spatially explicit forest cover change map for the Bosque Palmer strata (palms) for the entire time series. As explained in section B.1.1., the country is in the process of developing such a map, which will be available by 2017. This part is one of future improvements under the step-wise approach described in the FREL.

Regarding the consistency of the scope of the analysis of the activity data, the Technical Analysis team made an observation that the forest area classified as non-forest at the beginning of the period was not necessarily regenerating forests were exchanged on previously deforested areas in the period of analysis of the FREL and this could lead to a double counting if the results because they were accounted for as new deforestations in the results period.

Paraguay's response has been not to account for changes that occur outside the forest cover, since the changes are not related to the activity. The Technical Analysis team notes that the data provided by Paraguay for the analysis of the area classified as non-forest or change during the FREL will be analyzed in the near future and the results will be included in the final report on the technical assessment.

Additionally, it is highlighted that reforestation areas (which can be harvested and subsequently replanted) are not included in the activity data provided by Paraguay.

Solving this limitation is included as part of the improvement plan objectives presented earlier in this proposal.

Also, Paraguay has clarified that since the country does not have a definition and methodology to assess forest degradation, it cannot be analyzed if the changes occur in an area of forest cover that was previously degraded.

The technical report in regard to transparency, consistency, completeness and accuracy mentioned:

* **(a) Accuracy of the data:** The team did not provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Analysis Report in relation to the accuracy of the data and information provided in the technical annexes. However, the team notes that the analysis was based on the information related to significant issues raised in the Technical Analysis Report and whether these were raised and included in the analysis or not does not affect the accuracy of the data and results presented in the Technical Analysis Report, that were not material to the accuracy of the data on results and that could not be resolved due to lack of information.

As explained above, one of the observations from the Technical Analysis related to the possible double counting of non-forest areas or changes that could meet the forest definition and subsequently be delimited again. As a result of this, in a multi-year analysis, to be carried out during the forest area, we are used to address this issue.

The technical assessment report noted that Paraguay provided information on the estimation of the activity reflecting the impact of deforestation and reforestation under the step-wise approach.

The results of the 2015-2017 analysis assume that deforestation takes place in native forest and that instant occurrences occur; this is the same approach followed for the FREL. Forest degradation or the subsequent land use is not considered.

Based on the LULUCF experts noted that, if the carbon densities in these areas had been reduced (e.g., due to forest fires) or increased (e.g., due to natural regeneration or restoration) before a deforestation event then emissions from deforestation could have been either overestimated or underestimated.

Paraguay has clarified that the country does not have information on the carbon density of land used in the mask to track deforestation and that the intention is to include other REDD+ activities (degradation and enhancement) as part of the future improvements. The LULUCF experts also noted that not considering the carbon content of the land in its subsequent land use may lead to an overestimation of the emissions from deforestation and its corresponding reduction.

The Government of Paraguay has not received any payment for the period from 2015 to 2017, in addition, no request was made by other potential sources of payments, nor are there pending processes for periods before or after 2015-2017.

Treatment of the results paid by the GCF:

The Government of Paraguay is in the process of establishing a national registry of mitigation actions that will include emission reductions from REDD+ results. A provisional version of the registry is available online at the following link:

[http://doc.climates.org.py/registro-de-reduccion-de-emisiones-en-paraguay](http://doc.climates.org.py/registro-de-reduccion-de-emisiones-en-paraguay)

The final version of the registry is expected to be available before March 2020. This registry will be published and made publicly available in accordance with the provisions contained in the GCF Agreement, a pertinent instrument that allows transparency and traceability of emission reductions and payments received based on the 2016 FREL.

The volume of results presented to the GCF have been published in the "Lista REDD+ Information Hub" of the REDD plus Registry. This publication was made available on December COP-19.

In order to eliminate any possibility of double payments, there will be no transactions except for the GCF until a final registry is established and made publicly available.

### Financing that contributes to the results achieved

The Government of Paraguay has received financial support for REDD+ results. However, the country has implemented projects and actions have been planned that contribute to generating emission reductions in the LULUCF sector. These actions are not included in the registry but have contributed to the country's efforts in relation to REDD.

Likewise, the Government of Paraguay has received different types of international technical cooperation for the generation of results from REDD+ projects and actions carried out or planned by the country for REDD (Readiness Phase). These include the UN-REDD National Programme, which advanced in the strengthening of institutional capacity; the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) under the CDM Readiness Framework, and the Forests for Sustainable Growth Program of the FCPF that has financed the ENBCS (National Strategy for Biodiversity Conservation).

Other specific support received by MACDES and INFONFA corresponds to the technical cooperation of the Government of Japan (Forest Preservation) that contributed to the generation of alometric equations, support from the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) in the preparation of the National Forest Inventory and the preparation of the Biennial Update Reports and National Communications.

These multilateral or bilateral cooperation projects do not correspond to payments for REDD+ results.

There are several incentives already underway by means of Law 42273. These include the exemption of real estate taxes for plantations and forests in lands that have been designated as forest; the effect of income taxes being reduced to 5% on timber production; and exemptions from taxes on equipment needed for afforestation and reforestation activities.

REDD+ Projects under Voluntary Schemes in Paraguay

In Paraguay, there are several voluntary carbon market projects that include REDD+ activities of reducing deforestation and forest degradation. These projects have international certifications of the Voluntary Carbon Standard:

The first project was developed in the Department of Alto Paraná in the Dry Chaco and has an implementation period from 2011 to 2030. The projected estimated reductions or removals of emissions is 198,236.00 tCO2e for 20 years of the project.

The second project was developed in the Atlantic Forest (Reserva Hidroeléctrica de la Región Oriental) and covers the period from 2015-2011 to 2029-2030, with an estimated reduction or removals of emissions of 25.306.00 tCO2e for 20 years of the project.

Note: The above projects are not aligned with specific objectives of projects for REDD+ results and have not used the HLEF as a framework for reducing emissions.

### C. Carbon safeguards

#### C.1. Cancun safeguards

##### C.1.1. Compliance with Cancun safeguards

The implementation of policies and measures (PAMs) that contribute to the reduction of deforestation are based on the development processes proposed by the National Development Plan 2030, in coherence with the National Strategy for Forests for Sustainable Growth (ENBCS), the National Contributions of Paraguay, and other relevant laws and regulations.

The ENBCS is a strategy that builds on the country's development processes and has a concrete approach to sustainable forest management. It is based on the principles of participatory planning and long-term development. ENBCS PAMs are consistent with the National Climate Change Plan and the National Mitigation Strategy, which is focused on mainstreaming actions related to mitigating the adverse effects of


---
**Page 31**
---

climate change. In addition, the ENBCS and other related actions are consistent with relevant international conventions and agreements.

From the 2015-2017 period result of this proposal, the following activities, among others, were carried out:

* Analysis of the necessary legal aspects to implement REDD+ at the national level was carried out.
* From 2015 onwards, the development process of the National Climate Change Mitigation Strategy was under way.
* The development of the ENBCS was aligned with the relevant current legal framework for the implementation of REDD+, taking into account the relevant forest elements, including international instruments and conventions.

The ENBCS contributes to the country's sustainability development goals, as defined in the National Development Plan 2013-2017. The ENBCS is consistent with other national strategies and plans, including those defined in the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan, among others. The First Summary of Progress Report on the Implementation of REDD+ in Paraguay (2015) was presented to the Government. This document includes detailed information that demonstrates the complementarity and/or consistency of REDD+ actions in Paraguay with other national strategies and plans.

The ENBCS is also consistent with the National Forest Policy, which aims at reducing deforestation, with forest resource management and international conventions and agreements on the subject.

Two key pillars of the National Development Plan 2013-2017 are poverty reduction and social development and inclusive economic growth. These are directly related to the objectives of REDD+ in terms of poverty alleviation, social inclusion, and sustainable development. The ENBCS contributes to these goals by increasing income from the sale of environmental services, and increased efficiency in agricultural production systems.

The National Forest Policy was presented in 2009 with the objective of achieving sustainable socioeconomic development through the conservation of forest resources and their ecological and beneficial economic benefits of goods and services from the country's forests. REDD+ is consistent with Law No. 1737 "Forest Law".

Law 2524/04 on the prohibition of activities of transformation and conversion of areas with forest cover into agricultural land, in the Eastern Region of the country, and establishes a monitoring on the conversion of forests to other uses. This law was extended in 2006 (by Law 3198), 2013 (by Law 2063) and 2018 (by Law 2545).

Law 3001/06 "Compensation of Environmental Services".

Law 3001/06 "Compensation of Environmental Services", which has the objective of promoting the conservation, recovery and sustainable development of the country's biological diversity and natural resources.

The ENBCS and the implementation of PAMs to reduce deforestation are consistent with relevant international conventions and agreements on the rights of Indigenous Peoples and the American Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples. In addition to the following:

* Law 2519/03 approving the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.
* Law 2487/06 approving the National Forest Policy in Paraguay, in countries experiencing serious drought and/or desertification.
* Law 2543/03 approving the Constitution of the Republic of Paraguay (Part of José, Costa Rica).
* Law 2549/03 International Labor Organization Indigenous and Tribal Peoples Convention, 1989 (ILO Convention No. 169).
* Law 2539/03 approving the Convention on Biodiversity Articles (1, 2, 3, 7, 9).
* Law 2487/06 approving the National Forest Policy.
* Law 5837/86 approving and ratifying the Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora (CITES).
* Law 5652 approving the International Convention on Civil and Political Rights (ICCP).

REDDO-rBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND (Page 20 of 83)**

Paraguay is preparing for and promoting the implementation of REDD+ under the leadership of the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources Development (MADER), with the co-ordination of the National Secretariat for Economic and Social Development Planning (STP), the National Forestry Institute (INFONA), the National Forest Service (SINAFOR), and the support of the UN-REDD Programme and the Forest Carbon Co-operative Fund (FCCF). The process is also supported by the participation of indigenous peoples, local communities, and civil society organizations. This proposal is part of the National Strategy of Forests for Sustainable Growth (ENBCS), through a process that involved various institutions and stakeholders in the country.

The cross-cutting objective of ENBCS is to strengthen institutional capacities to articulate and coordinate forest governance at all levels.

In the context of REDDO in Paraguay, there is a commitment to an effective forest governance framework, that takes into account the relevant regulatory framework and national sovereignty. There is also a commitment to advancing transparency and accountability for forest-related information, including access to public access to information. There is also a commitment to making previous notice mechanisms available during the process of decision-making.

The First Summary of Information on Addressing and Respecting REDD+ Safeguards in Paraguay for the period 2011-2018 includes detailed information on the legal framework in Paraguay relevant for promoting transparency, accountability, and respect for human rights and indigenous peoples' rights, as well as national sovereignty. Of particular relevance is the Law 5382/14 on Free Citizen Access to Public Information and Government Records, which provides citizens with a right of access to public information and government records, including those related to REDD+ processes. Under this Law, any citizen can request information and data in various formats from public entities, including the National Forest Service (SINAFOR), within 10 working days after which they have specific deadlines for responding to requests.

During the outcome period for this proposal (2015-2017), the activities described as follows were carried out, in accordance with the national strategy of forest governance and environmental governance:

MADER, the REDDO focal point for the UNFCCC, has implemented actions as an environmental authority with respect to the national laws under its jurisdiction, contributing to the actions of other institutions such as INFONA, SINAFOR, STP, and others. MADER has also been involved in the implementation of the National Forest Policy, has actively participated in the development of REDDO-PAM, both at the national level and with the support of the UN-REDD Programme. MADER has also worked to strengthen criminal procedures of environmental and forest law violations to achieve effective sanctions that strengthen law enforcement and promote compliance.

In this period, the process of establishing a land tenure formalization mechanism was initiated. Several actions were also implemented focusing on institutional strengthening, aimed at better forest control and monitoring, forest management, and sustainable development.

Documents related to the REDDO in Paraguay and the ENBCS are available online to the general public.

Several government institutions, including MADER, have made efforts to have information digitized and made publicly available. There are also initiatives that can support REDD+ implementation, making it easier for the public to make informed decisions on forest-related issues. These include the National Forest Service (SINAFOR) website with information, including the Unified Public Information Portal, which has 383 member institutions. As an example, 22 requests were received from civil society organizations and other stakeholders through the unified portal between 2015 and 2017. Overall, MADER received 34 requests in 2016 (14 women, 19 men and 1 not declared) and 83 requests in 2017 (40 women, 36 men and 1 not declared). These requests included confidential information on forest-related issues such as land tenure, procedures, environmental impact studies/assessments, and climate change reports, among others.

According to records in the period 2015-2018, the Department of Integrated Environmental Auditing carried out 183 auditing and intervention procedures on issues related to deforestation, land-use changes, selective tree extraction, illegal logging, and other environmental crimes. These responses were made with support from institutions and tax offices, and ex officio checks. These responses are all in the current context of environmental crime and forest management. The information obtained as a result of these interventions on the interventions was then sent to the Legal Advisory Directorate of MADES for them to take the corresponding actions against those responsible.

INFONA also has mechanisms for reporting on corruption and forest-related issues. There is an option for anonymous reporting.

A diagnostic report and a study were carried out on the development of a grievance redress mechanism associated with REDD+ in Paraguay, which will allow complaints and comments regarding the ENBCS to be filed at all REDD+ related issues in this country. This mechanism will be validated at a national level and then implemented.

During the implementation of the UN-REDD National Joint Programme (2011-2016) an e-mail account (ocorrencia@pmy@gmail.com) was set up to receive, review and process concerns and recommendations, responding to them in a timely manner. This mechanism is still active today.

At the REDD+ governance level in Paraguay, beyond the national forest governance institutions mentioned above, in the UN-REDD National Programme "a National Technical Team" comprising members of SEAM (now MINAE), MADES, and INAC was formed to coordinate the implementation of the UN-REDD National Joint Programme Development Programme (UNDP) and the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) was formed, and met regularly with the National Coordinating Committee for Forests and Biodiversity.

Within the framework of the Forests for Sustainable Growth (BGS) Project, which involves MADES, INAC and UNDP, a National Technical Committee has been created to coordinate the implementation of this project in sustainable development. This Committee is operating in working groups focused on the activities of the project.

The National REDD+ Focal Point of Paraguay requested that the Secretary of the National Commission on Climate change set up a "REDD+" Roundtable, composed of members of this Committee as well as other actors, including indigenous peoples' representatives, civil society associations, academia and the private sector.

Paraguay is home to 17 indigenous peoples and Households of 2012 identified 433 indigenous communities, with 19 different indigenous groups, grouped into five linguistic families. According to the Census, indigenous peoples live in 13 departments of the country, with a total population of 117,150 people.

The recognition and protection of ancestral knowledge and the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities is framed in Chapter V of the Constitution of the Republic of Paraguay, which recognizes the essential role that these groups play in the conservation of natural resources and biodiversity.

Paraguayan State: Indigenous peoples have the right to collective ownership of land, in sufficient extent and quality for their cultural and economic development. The law establishes a series of safeguards and guarantees for safeguarding, local communities are defined as those who are dependent on forests and small farmers (campesinos).

There are several other elements of the Paraguayan legal framework which also strongly emphasize the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities: recognition of their heritage, traditions, and rights for the improvement of their living conditions.

REDD+ Safeguards in Paraguay for the period 2011-2018 includes detailed information on the relevant legal frameworks that apply to REDD+ activities in Paraguay:

* **Law No.4943: Indigenous Communities Statute.**
    * Law 2128/03 ratifies the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination (ICERD).
    * ILO Convention No. 169 concerning Indigenous and Tribal Peoples in Independent Countries, which is recognized as a fundamental human right.
    * United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples and the American Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.

In addition, Decree 1038/14 approved "the Protocol for the Process of Consultation and Free, Prior and Informed Consent of Indigenous Peoples Living in Paraguay". This Decree is the result of nine years of consultations and preparation between various government agencies and representatives of indigenous communities. The Decree also includes a reference to the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples' Constitution, mentioned above, as well as Convention No. 169, Article 6, which stipulates that in Paraguay's 2012 Indigenous Peoples' Census, an indigenous community was defined as "a group or set of indigenous families and/or individuals who share a common origin, culture, language, and social organization, and who are interested in geographic terms, but also in social terms, which means that groups of indigenous peoples living in estancias, urban or rural neighborhoods, among others, which should be identified".

Government shall consult the peoples concerned, through appropriate procedures, whenever susceptible legislation or administrative measures are envisaged that may affect them. This includes the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples, the American Convention on Human Rights and other international instruments applicable to Paraguay. The Government will also seek the advice of the Paraguayan Indigenous Institute to issue relevant regulations for the effective implementation of the provision, with the cooperation of indigenous peoples.

During the process of developing the ENBCS, FAPR included indigenous organizations as key partners in decision-making and inter-institutional work, first with FAPR as a part of the UN REDD Programme, and with INDI as part of the project "Indigenous Peoples and Forests".

A number of activities were developed during this time to promote the involvement of indigenous peoples in REDD+, including: (i) the identification of principles, operational guidelines and work plan to Promote the Effective Participation of Indigenous Peoples in REDD+; (ii) the development of a methodology for assessing cultural benefits associated with sites considered suitable by different communities, as well as evaluating and mapping the potential environmental services "REDD+" associated with the livelihoods of indigenous peoples and local communities (see also Section C.2.6).

There have been several efforts to ensure the involvement of indigenous actors in REDD+ and to consider their voices in the design and implementation of the ENBCS. In this regard, FAPR has ensured that indigenous actors were involved in the process of developing the ENBCS with the objective of incorporating their visions and needs. FAPR developed its own information system on indigenous lands and territories in Paraguay, which is now online.

Support has been provided in processes of land titling and recognition of collective property. Within the framework of the National Programme for the Titling of Indigenous Lands (PNTI), a significant step forward was made on the certification of forests of several indigenous communities through a system of voluntary agreements with private landowners.

In addition, small farmer (campesino) communities have been identified as important stakeholders for REDD+. ENBCS development has included several specific spaces for dialogue and discussion with this stakeholder group.

## Comments:

### (iv) The full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities, in the actions referred to in paragraphs 70 and 72 of TCP.16

Comments: Full and effective participation in the context of REDD+ by Paraguay is understood as the participation of all relevant stakeholders who can influence or be affected by REDD+, both in the design and implementation of the ENBCS and specific PAMs. The Paraguayan State has the mission to ensure the rights of participation of women, men, youth, children, indigenous peoples, local communities and other vulnerable groups with access the benefits of nature. Also, indigenous peoples have the right to be consulted on any project that may affect them.

The Constitution of the Republic of Paraguay recognizes the rights of indigenous peoples and allows for their free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) in this regard. Decree 1039/15 approved by the National Congress establishes a process of consultation with "Indigenous Peoples Living in Paraguay" as applied (see Section II). Consultation is an obligation of the Paraguayan State, which must be carried out through the Indigenous People's Institute.

The Constitution of the Republic of Paraguay includes the right to "the defense of diffuse interests" (Article 38), which establishes that every person has the right to defend measures against the defense of the environment, the integrity and quality of life. The State is obliged to ensure the protection of these rights in accordance with the principles of consultation and participation.

The Constitution of Paraguay also includes a provision concerning indigenous peoples by law independent from Congress, by Law 23490, the Paraguayan Indigenous Institute (INDI) is established as an autonomous institution for the promotion and defense of the rights of indigenous peoples concerned, through appropriate procedures and in particular through their representative institutions, whenever legislative or administrative measures may affect them and/or endanger their existence.

The creation of INDI is based on the principles and guidelines of the National Development Plan 2030, which seeks to achieve gender equality as well as equitable development. The ENBCS seeks to
