In this context, the scientific and excluded actions will be analyzed based on the environmental-social-economic dimension and its contribution to sustainability.

The above-mentioned ESFS Framework will apply safeguard standards with the aim of anticipating and managing environmental, social and economic problems in a holistic way. This is a requirement for any excluded action under REDD+ plus RBP funding proposals. The key elements of the safeguards, as well as the use of proceeds, embedded in the ESFS procedures, can be summarized as follows (see UNEP-ESFS Framework, Chapter 5).

* **Environmental, social and economic screening (or scoping), using a Environmental, Social and Economic Screening Tool (ESST)**
* **Preparation of safeguard assessment and management (i.e. gender action, indigenous peoples, resettlement, etc.) plans for each project**
* **Mitigation, management and monitoring of impacts during project implementation ensuring that the added value of REDD+ plus RBP funding is maximized**

Procedures and requirements have been defined to ensure a prompt and effective response to environmental and social-economic problems that may arise.

The risk assessment will be conducted in accordance with pre-established formats, guidelines, and procedures, based on relevant national circumstances, and for which the participation of key actors in the entire risk management cycle is essential. The risk assessment will be carried out at the beginning of the project to support and guide the implementation of all activities. It is expected that these plans will provide a basis for the development of safeguard plans, including the identification of risks, the development of stakeholder involvement processes, indigenous peoples participation, and coordination, gender mainstreaming, etc.

The safeguards plans to be developed at the start of this project will put emphasis on the determination of preventive measures to avoid or mitigate adverse effects related to biodiversity conservation, natural habitats, and associated ecosystem services. The project will also ensure that all activities are in line with national legislation and international scientific certainty. When assessing and managing risks related to biodiversity conservation, natural habitats, and associated ecosystem services, the following considerations will apply during activity specific assessments during project inception:

* **identification of critical or natural habitats or semi-natural habitats;**
* **assessment of the potential impact on these habitats;**
* **avoidance of significant conversion or degradation in habitats that are legally protected, officially proposed for protection, recognized as having high conservation value, or recognized as protected by traditional local communities, where national legislation so allows;**
* **ensuring that any activities will not have a significant adverse effect on the biodiversity and ecosystem services for which the critical habitat was designated;**
* **avoidance of conversion or degradation in habitats that are home to a significant population of any critically endangered or endangered species;**
* **ensuring that no activities will be carried out in areas where these services are one or more of the following:**
    * evidence of impact through identification and protection of set-asides
    * minimizing impacts on biodiversity and ecosystem services by ensuring that all land use changes are planned, managed and monitored to avoid adverse effects.
* **sting infrastructure investments on lands where natural habitats have already been converted naturally or artificially;**
* **providing biodiversity and ecosystem services offsets.**
* **ensuring that any activities will not have a significant adverse impact on the organic recognition management plans;**
* **avoidance of activities with adverse impacts on soils, their organic content, productivity, structure and water retention capacity, as to contribute to improving land degradation.**

### Stakeholder Engagement in Activity-Level Environmental and Social Impact Assessment

The project will conduct an activity-level environmental and social impact assessment, which will cover the following:

- **Stakeholder engagement under UNFCCC Climate Change Fund**: This will include a definition of the role of different stakeholders in activity design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation.
- **Participation of stakeholders in the application and reporting on applicable safeguards frameworks**, including the REDD+ Safeguards Information System (SIS).
- **Focus on stakeholder engagement in environmental and forest law reform and enforcement** (e.g., participation in consultations with indigenous peoples regarding land tenure rights).
- **Stakeholder engagement in the design and implementation of a communication strategy**

The stakeholder engagement plan will inform the development of an indigenous peoples plan that will include standard operating procedures for the project, including the identification of key stakeholders, the process of provision of FPIC of indigenous peoples in Papua, ensuring that traditional land use rights, and access to land are respected. The plan will also address the role of indigenous peoples in project design, activity design, implementation, and monitoring (see section E5).

The project will also ensure that accessible, effective and independent mechanisms for grievance redress are operational at all levels of the project. These mechanisms will be informed and guided by international good practices enshrined in the UNEP ESFS Framework. Under this framework an independent ombudsman will be appointed to oversee the operation of these mechanisms and provide oversight of the stakeholder response mechanism. Any complaints can be raised and communicated through the project communication strategy, including via a dedicated email address.

- **A compliance review process** to respond in cases where UNEP may not be in compliance with its own ESFS Framework.
- **A grievance redress mechanism** that provides people affected by UNEP projects access to appropriate and flexible dispute resolution procedures.

Project-level grievance redress procedures will be accessible to all stakeholders (being age- and gender-inclusive, culturally sensitive, and reflecting cultural diversity) and ensure transparency and processes, and include procedures to:

- Keep the public informed about project implementation.
- Receive and register communications, concerns and complaints from the public.
- Promptly assess issues raised and determine how to resolve them;
- Ensure that all parties are kept informed of progress on resolving their concerns or complaints;
- Document responses to public communications, concerns and complaints
- Make appropriate adjustments to the design of the project (particularly the safeguards plans), and
- Enable continuous learning from ongoing improvements to the grievance redress mechanism.

To ensure indigenous peoples, local communities and other marginalized groups can access the grievance redress procedures, the project will ensure that:

- **Stakeholder responses** and grievance redress mechanisms will be identified or designed in consultation with the affected community or affected communities of indigenous peoples;
- The mechanisms will be sufficiently accessible and readily available to all parties (e.g., individuals, communities, and organizations) who may have concerns about the project. The grievance procedures will utilize existing formal or informal procedures, supplemented by project-specific arrangements.
- Language barriers will be taken into account, and there will be provision for interpretation when and where possible;
- Complaints will be processed in a confidential manner, especially in instances where the complainants fear retaliation; and
- Concerns, grievances and responses will be registered and documented, and made available to the public.

Basic information required to access the grievance redress mechanism will include:

- Actual or perceived negative economic, environmental or social impact on an individual or group,
- Or concern about the potential of project activities to cause such impact.

### E.2 Risk assessment

#### E.2.1 For the period of the achieved results

Paraguay has strong support for respecting individual and collective rights as provided by the country's Constitution, laws, regulations, and other legal instruments. The country has put in place policies and measures in a transparent, efficient and participatory way. As part of the country's legal framework, the Law on Access to Information (LAI) was enacted in 2013. This law provides for public access to public management and to guarantee access to information related to administrative and government acts. Also, LAI establishes the National Public Information System (SINIF), which is a tool that supports Government transparency has provided important support for transparency and accountability.

Moreover, the Anti-Corruption Secretariat (SENAIC) is the governing, normative and strategic entity in charge of the country's anti-corruption policy. The Secretariat is responsible for formulating national policies on anti-corruption, integrity, and transparency. The National Anti-Corruption Office is a specialized technical body that provides support to the Secretariat in the implementation of the country’s anti-corruption regulations and other legislative and/or administrative norms that may be dictated, for the direction, supervision, coordination, execution and evaluation of programs, projects, plans and activities within its sphere of competence.

All these elements along with the recognition of the importance of risk analysis and management, have provided support to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals. While the risk analysis exercises performed do not specify a difference between internal and external risks, the results of the risk tolerance levels exercise indicate that residual risk tolerance levels were not exceeded, neither where there any negative impacts that could be attributed to the project.

There have also been established platforms for requesting and accessing information, making complaints, and promoting transparent accountability. Over the past few years, the country has allocated resources to promote transparency in public administration through the implementation of a number of reforms aimed at improving administrative, financial and technical processes that contribute to sustainable development goals. ## The environmental and social risks and impacts related to the context of REDD+ in Paraguay

The environmental and social risks and impacts related to the context of REDD+ in Paraguay were captured in the ESS Framework (see section 1.2). These risks and impacts were implemented in a way consistent with requirements of the GCF ESST standards (see section E.11).

There is a strong commitment to avoid, minimize or mitigate environmental, social and economic risks associated with REDD+ implementation in Paraguay. The project will implement measures that are designed to safeguard standards during the project preparation phase, as well as design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation phases.

The initial project preparation phase includes a due diligence process, including scoping and more detailed environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) of the proposed activities. This ESIA will include an analysis and management approach appropriate to the potential risks and to direct, indirect, cumulative and associated impacts that may arise from the implementation of REDD+ in Paraguay.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) [PAGE 10 OF 83]

## Restrictions on access to land or use of resources in protected areas that are sources of livelihoods.

Despite all efforts to avoid loss of access to land and natural resources, the project could, however, lead to full or partial displacement of communities living within or adjacent to such areas. The potential for displacement as a result of project activities will be analysed in detail during the project inception phase through an extensive resettlement risk assessment (RRA) process. This RRA will form part of the project’s environmental and social action plan, developed in close consultation with affected stakeholders and in line with the government protocol on REDD+ projects. The RRA will identify areas where displacement is likely to occur, assess potential impacts, compensate losses, and provide benefits to persons and communities voluntarily resettling as a consequence of project activities.

### Indicative compensation processes will involve:

- Informing potentially affected persons of their rights, consulting them on options available, and providing them with economically feasible livelihood alternatives and technical assistance;
- Providing information about the project’s potential impacts in relation to access to land prior to project before land clearing, construction, or access restrictions begin;
- Providing financial compensation for loss of income and assets, including livelihood development and restoration assistance;
- Compensating persons for loss of land when livelihoods of directly affected persons are land-based, or where land is in collective ownership;
- The provision of alternative housing if the project has a significant impact on overall livelihoods as assessed relatively minor; 
- Including access to basic civic infrastructure and community services at all residential sites of resettlement
- Transparent compensation procedures and standards applied consistently to all directly affected persons;
- Displacement not occurring until compensation has been made available and, where applicable, necessary alternative housing is provided.

### UNEP will ensure that communities and persons directly affected by planned resettlement are engaged in the planning and decision-making processes, as well as during implementation and monitoring of resettlement. There is a strong emphasis on ensuring that displacement is avoided or minimized to the greatest extent possible; avoided, and only carefully planned resettlement considered in cases of legal enforcement of REDD+ actions.

The safeguards plan to be developed at the beginning of the project will emphasize the determination of processes for identifying persons who may be displaced as well as those who are likely to experience adverse impacts related to practices specified in the Green Climate Fund policy dealing with this issue. Further information is provided on indicative resettlement and other safeguards plans in the ESMP, which is included at the end of this proposal.

## E.3 Gender considerations

### E.3.1 For the period of the achieved results

Further information will be provided in the document describing the extent to which the measures undertaken complied with the GCF gender policy.

The design and implementation of REDD+ policies and measures in Paraguay are based on a gender mainstreaming approach, which is consistent with the objectives of the Green Climate Fund, and other objectives of different projects and programmes, including the UN-REDD Paraguay National Programme and the Global Environment Facility (GEF) project. The GCF has adopted a gender policy that seeks to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment, with efforts to incorporate approaches for addressing gender inequality in all its activities. In addition, the activities proposed are consistent with the UNEP policy on gender, which emphasizes the need to ensure that gender considerations are integrated into all aspects of project design and implementation. The GCF also has a specific objective to obtain gender equity results at different levels: (i) Results-based management for gender equity using indicators and protocols for evaluation; (ii) Supervision through monitoring, evaluation, audits, and reporting; (iii)


---
**Page 74**
---

REDDO-rbp FUNDING PROPOSAL
GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE (13 of 63)

human and financial resources; vi) capacity building in gender mainstreaming, coherence, knowledge and information sharing.

Paraguay is signatory of several international conventions [7] that focus on gender equality and women's empowerment, including CEDAW, a United Nations convention that aims at eliminating any form of discrimination against women. The Convention also promotes the principle of non-discrimination, explicitly establishing equality of persons, equality of rights between women and men and non-Discrimination in all its forms. However, despite these international commitments, Paraguay has not yet made equal status to become real and effective, removing obstacles that impede or hinder its implementation and facilitating the full enjoyment by women of their human rights.

Paraguay created a Women's Secretariat in 1992, which became the Women's Ministry in 2017. It also developed a National Plan for Equal Opportunities between Women and Men for the period 2008-2017.

The National Development Plan 2030, which guides the ENBCS, seeks to achieve gender equity as well as equitable access to natural resources.

The UNREDD National Programme of Paraguay identified in its evaluation of key actions for REDD+, the following cross-cutting themes for the dialogue processes related to REDD+: the promotion of the gender approach and the participation of women in decision-making on forest-related issues, including access to natural resources. The National Program's participation and communication strategy aims at facilitating an equitable distribution of benefits from forests among all actors involved, taking into account different levels, persons, genders and cultures, and to implement mechanisms to achieve gender equity and reduce inequalities.

Based on the mapping of actions, Paraguay has worked on the consolidation of the plan for the participation and involvement of actors from 2015 onwards. The Key Stakeholder Participation Plan Part of the Forests for Sustainable Development Programme (PESD) is a participatory process that seeks to ensure gender equality in decision-making processes related to forests.

The ENBCS, Paraguay seeks to achieve gender equity through the participation of women in all areas of forest management and conservation. The National Program's strategy aims at reducing gender inequality by providing access to credit for women involved in the implementation of REDD+ activities, taking into account disaggregated data collected in the implementation phase, as well as socio-economic criteria.

Since forests are a key element in Paraguay's biodiversity, they play an important role in the country's survival. The ENBCS integrates multiple aspects, policies, and institutions involved in the protection of forests.

Based on the recognition of the need to strengthen actions on gender and climate change, the National Gender Strategy (SGN) was developed by the Ministry of Women and Children in 2015. This strategy actively incorporates the gender perspectives into initiatives related to climate change "in order to ensure that new policies and programs are designed with a gender perspective." The SGN is based on four strategic pillars: (1) awareness raising, communication and training; (2) financial management; (3) knowledge management and technology; and those strategic pillars are all framed within the key priority areas of the "Green Climate Fund's policy path."

In addition, several activities were implemented with the objective of involving men and women in the preparation and implementation of REDD+ in the country, which included:

- Involvement of men, women and other relevant groups in environmental and social risk identification exercises and on the identification of possible environmental and social benefits related to REDD+ implementation in the country.
- Participation of men and women in workshops and meetings that aimed at identifying REDD+ policies measures and actions and the framework for the construction of the ENBCS. This process sought to identify and consider gender perspectives, needs and priorities in the design and implementation of REDD+.
- Compilation of information on the participation of men and women in forms using formats that considered disaggregation by sex. This was not done in a standardized manner, but it is a recommendation that will influence future actions.
- Training key actors on issues related to forests, climate change, and REDD+.
- Analysis of gaps in the inclusion of the gender approach and generation of recommendations. The following should be highlighted:
  - Sustainable production systems: encouraging women's participation in prioritizing technical assistance.
  - Sustainable management of forests through the promotion and integration of local ancestral knowledge to the development of their livelihoods and environmentally friendly produce, bringing forward the role of women as key actors in the conservation of natural resources and biodiversity in local communities and indigenous peoples, in the domestic use of firewood from native forests.
  - Adoption of deep-rooted, sound policies: strengthening the access of women's groups to formal credit facilities.
  - Development of capacities for environmental regulation, control, monitoring, and penalization promoting the role of women in forest monitoring and protection processes.

The Environmental and Social Assessment conducted to present this funding proposal determined that the risks associated with gender are not specific to the project but rather are more general and common foreseen in risk identification exercises, and therefore, only general measures to mitigate these risks have been developed. This study will be used as a reference for the development of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) and the gender gaps and roles, as inputs for the formulation and implementation of REDD+ PAMs. This gap will be addressed in the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), which is currently being prepared by the BCS project, and a similar framework defined for this proposal. The Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) will include specific measures to mitigate gender risks at the start of the project and the construction of a safeguard plan, which will include a section on gender with a clear action plan, with minimum criteria, activities and specific monitoring indicators.

### 3.2.3. For the use of the process:

Provide adequate and sufficient information on how the AE will undertake activity-level gender assessments and evaluations as part of the project implementation.

Gender equality and women's empowerment are recognized as cross-cutting priority issues in all aspects of UN Environment’s work. The participation of men and women in environmental protection and sustainable development is essential for achieving environmental sustainability, ensuring that environmental policies and programs are means of achieving environmental sustainability, and to incorporating a gender approach in the design and implementation of projects.

The promotion and contribution to gender equity will be specifically guided by two policies: The UN System-Wide Action Plan on Gender Equity and the Empowerment of Women provides a framework for mainstreaming gender equality into all aspects of UN system work. The Gender Equality Policy, which can be taken into account as part of the ESMP, is an important tool to ensure that the activities of this project are in line with the following principles: (i) ensuring accountability for gender equity at different levels within the organization; (ii) promoting gender equality through management and staff development; (iii) ensuring sound management for gender equity among producers and workers; (iv) establishing oversight through monitoring, evaluation, auditing and reporting; (v) allocating sufficient human and financial resources.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (Page 10 of 83)

### v) capacity building for gender mainstreaming; vi) ensuring coherence, knowledge and information management.

Considering its institutional mandate, UNEP has the responsibility to contribute to the achievement of gender equality in environmental assessments, analyses, standards, guidelines and methods used to promote sustainable development. The REDD+ rBP project will ensure that all activities are designed so that they do not discriminate against women or reinforce discrimination or inequalities based on gender, and implement a gender action plan as part of its overall strategy.

The project will contribute to the implementation of Paraguay’s National Gender Strategy on Climate Change (Estrategia Nacional de Género ante el Cambio Climático). The strategy actively incorporates gender equality in all aspects of climate change policy. It is based on four pillars: (1) access and financing; (2) education, communication and citizen participation; (3) knowledge management and technology.

In addition to hiring a gender specialist for the Project Management Unit, which will help to ensure gender mainstreaming throughout the project cycle, the project will also conduct an initial gender assessment at the activity level, and develop action plans at the project level according to identified needs and to the gender equality strategy.

Within the context of continuous management, risks will be continually evaluated and monitored, and actions taken to avoid or mitigate them. In line with the gender assessment and action plans, and also Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs), a gender impact assessment will be conducted at key milestones. Evaluations and tests for continuous monitoring will be designed and implemented integrating gender equality considerations.

While the initial risk identification exercise summarized in ESA (see Annex) did not emphasize risks related to gender inequality gaps, the existence of these gaps in Paraguay is widely acknowledged, and addressing these gaps will be a priority throughout the project cycle. The project will therefore focus on strengthening existing activities in this proposal, in line with the elements identified above. Sustainable development cannot be achieved without ensuring that women’s voices are heard and their needs addressed as key priorities.

Therefore, it is critical to identify and implement actions that positively contribute to gender equality, including support in the implementation of Paraguay’s National Gender Strategy on Climate Change.

The document also includes an annex to this proposal.

## E.4. Interim policy on prohibited practices

### 4.1. For the period of the achieved results

Paraguay has a legal framework that is sufficient and adequate to demonstrate that no Prohibited Practices occurred during the implementation of the activities that lead to the REDD+ plus results, such as: undisclosed Prohibited Practices, illegal timber trade, money laundering, etc., and that all actions were carried out in accordance with the implementation of results-based actions, and double payment or financing for the same achievements achieved.

Paraguay has developed a legal framework in accordance with international treaties to prevent money laundering. The country has also implemented a National Strategy against Money Laundering, which was comprehensively implemented and has demonstrated significant changes in transparency and accountability of public administration. In this regard, Paraguay has been recognized by the United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC) as a country that has made progress in implementing the recommendations of its Executive Board called the Money Laundering Prevention Secretary ("Secretaría de Prevención del Lavado de Dinero y Financiamiento del Terrorismo" - SEPRELAD). The UNODC Executive Board also recognized the work carried out by the Executive Secretary of SEPRELAD, Mr. José Luis Sánchez, who was awarded the 2014 UNODC Award for Excellence in Anti-Money Laundering.

SEPRELAD's main objectives are to prevent and avoid the use of the financial system and other sectors of the economy for money laundering that comes from illegal activities. It carries out its actions in accordance with its obligations under international treaties, as well as the provisions contained in the Law on Money Laundering, the Regulation of Money or Goods, and also determine with precision the need for structural, legal, administrative changes among others.

The legal framework implemented is extensive; the most important laws underlying SEPRELAD's actions are:

1. http://www.paraguay.gob.py/documentos/2014/10/18/95607.pdf (Law of United Services, Peace and Action Plan 2014-2023)
2. http://www.seprelad.gov.py/


---
**Page 77**
---

Law 1015/97, "703/96 preventing and penalizing unlawful acts to launder money or property" and creating LAFIPLAR.
Law 3448/08, "modifying various provisions of law 1106/97 criminal code".
Law 2735/09, which amends several articles of Law No. 1015/97 that prevents and represses the REDD+ RBP.
Law 4024/10, "to cast the punishable facts of terrorism, terrorism association and financing of terrorism".
Law 638/11, which establishes the National Anti-Corruption Secretariat (SENAIC).
Decree 1594/12, which "establishes the National Anti-Corruption Secretariat" (SENAIC). This Decree is the result of a process of implementation, evaluation and evolution of the National Government's public policies on anti-corruption, integrity and transparency. It is a technical and specialized management body within the Office of the President of the Republic, which has the mission to promote the fight against corruption in accordance with regulations and other legislative and / or administrative rules that are enacted for the exercise of its powers, as well as to provide support and advice on the planning, implementation and activities within its own competencies.
Law 1200/13, "ratification of the United Nations Convention against Corruption". Article 5 establishes that each State Party shall, in accordance with the fundamental principles of its legal system, adopt or maintain such measures as may be necessary to ensure that public officials and other persons entrusted by law with functions relating to the management of public affairs or property make use of their powers only for the purposes intended thereby. Each State Party shall endeavor to establish and promote effective practices aimed at the prevention of corruption.

The related legislation, including all regulatory decrees, resolutions and circulars, are publicly available in SEPRELAD's website (www.seprelad.gov.py).

The Paraguayan government has also updated its legislation to conform to international requirements, such as the amendment of the Criminal Code (Law 1560/1997). It has developed and implemented a "Strategic Plan of the Paraguayan Government for the Prevention and Combating of Money Laundering and Financing of Terrorism" in accordance with Resolution No. 234/08, which was approved by Presidential Decree 12200 on 11 June 2013. Recommendations from the National Commission against Money Laundering and Financing of Terrorism were also considered and integrated into the Strategic Plan.

The validity of this plan as well as of existing legislation and its constant improvement guarantee that the Paraguayan Government has solid internal structures and regional collaboration mechanisms to avoid prohibited practices.


### 4.2. For the use of processes

Financial management and procurement information on control systems that assures that the procedures will be used in a manner compliant with the Interno Policy on Prohibited Practices, such as: undisclosed financial transactions, unauthorized payments, double payment or financing for the same results achieved; etc.

The financial management and procurement within the project will be guided by UN Financial Regulations, Rules and practices, as well as UNPES program manual. The Financial Rules of UNPES are promulgated pursuant to Resolution 10/2008 of the Executive Board of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and are managed as per UN rules and procedures, including eligibility criteria, proposal evaluation processes, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. Furthermore, the legal instruments to be signed with Executing Entities include warranties and covenants by the Executing Entities to enter into an amount

### E.S Indigenous peoples

Provide adequate and sufficient information on how the activities to be implemented with the use of proceeds, will be consistent with the rights of indigenous peoples and their cultural significance. The project will be guided by the recommendations of the Permanent Forum of the United Nations for Indigenous issues and the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP). The project will also be consistent with Paraguay's national laws and commitments, which will ensure that the activities to be implemented with the use of proceeds are in accordance with international law. The project will ensure that all activities implemented with the use of proceeds will be designed so that adverse impacts on indigenous peoples are mitigated or avoided.

The implementation of UNEP’s ESEES Framework and its specific safeguard on indigenous peoples (which is guided by the recommendations of the Permanent Forum of the United Nations for Indigenous issues and the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples) will ensure that all activities to be implemented with the use of proceeds are in accordance with international law. The project will ensure that all activities implemented with the use of proceeds will be consistent with the Green Climate Fund’s Indigenous Peoples Policy which seeks to ensure that activities are designed so as not to adversely affect indigenous peoples’ rights, including their land and territory, their dignity, their human rights and their cultural uniqueness, so that they receive appropriate social and economic benefits from climate change mitigation projects.

The project will be implemented in a way that respects the rights of indigenous peoples and takes into account their views, needs and rights, to avoid any harm and promote opportunities to improve their livelihoods. Adverse impacts on indigenous peoples are avoided or mitigated by ensuring that they have access to information about the project’s governance processes and procedures will be the focus of awareness-raising and capacity-building efforts. The project will also ensure that all activities implemented with the use of proceeds are in accordance with international conventions, as well as UNEP’s ESEES, several minimum criteria are explained below, which seek to mitigate or avoid adverse impacts on indigenous peoples.

The project will be designed so as not to adversely affect indigenous peoples’ rights, including their land and territory, their dignity, their human rights and their cultural uniqueness. The project will consider indigenous peoples as key partners, particularly through collaboration with FAP and INDI for:

- planning phase of the project, including the assessment of potential environmental and socioeconomic risks and impacts on indigenous peoples;
- identifying, selecting and implementing actions to avoid adverse impacts on indigenous peoples are evolved in a participatory manner by involving indigenous peoples’ representatives;
- defining who will serve as representative bodies of culturally diverse indigenous peoples, sharing their views, needs and rights with project stakeholders;
- applying and documenting the process for engaging indigenous peoples, according to international standards and best practices that involve the Protocol for FPIC in Paraguay; identifying opportunities to enable indigenous peoples benefit from the project in a culturally appropriate and feasible manner;
- agreeing on the terms of engagement with indigenous peoples through agreements with existing local government systems or self-government and cultural norms (see section 6.1.3);
- fully and effectively participating in the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of project activities; identifying aspects of key cultural heritage of indigenous people that needs to be protected throughout the implementation of project activities.

Law 2138/03 ratifies the Convention against all forms of racial discrimination.

Paraguay is a signatory to ILO’s Convention 169 concerning Indigenous and Tribal Peoples in Independent Countries, which falls within the framework of Law 23453 concerning Indigenous and Tribal Peoples, adopted on December 17th, 2008. This law also incorporates the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples and the American Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.

In addition, Decree 1039/18 approving the "Protocol for the Process of Consultation and Free, Prior and Informed Consent with Indigenous Peoples Living in Paraguay" is the result of nine years of consultation and preparation. The protocol was approved by the National Congress on December 27th, 2018, and published as an official regulation in 2018. This protocol is based on Chapter V of the Indigenous Peoples Constitution, as well as the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples, the International Labor Organization Convention on Human Rights and other international instruments ratified by Paraguay. This protocol authorizes the Paraguayan State to enter into agreements with indigenous peoples for the implementation or partial implementation of the provision, with the collaboration of indigenous peoples.

The protocol will apply, as appropriate, to the activities implemented within the framework of this proposal to ensure that activities are carried out in accordance with the principles and provisions established by the protocol.


Further information is provided in the ESFM document, which is included as an Annex to this proposal.



### E.E. Monitoring and evaluation

Provide information on the monitoring mechanisms that will take place for providing annual monitoring reports to the Project Steering Committee (PSC) and UNEP.

Monitoring and evaluation will follow established UNEP and GCF evaluation policies. The M&E plan will include an inception report, annual reports to GCF and UNEP, a mid-term review/evaluation, and terminal evaluation. The M&E plan will be developed during the project’s workshop including final agreement on indicators, means of verification, and M&E responsibilities.


### Project Inception Workshop

The inception workshop is designed to serve the following purposes:

- Detail and clearly roles and responsibilities of the different partners involved in implementation;
- Finalization of the first annual work plan;
- Validation of the monitoring and evaluation framework of verification;
- Finalization of the Gender Action Plan;
- Review of project risks, mitigation plans and protocols; and
- Formal launching of the project


### Annual reporting

Annual reports will be prepared by the project Management Unit and approved by Project Steering Committee. The format and content of the annual report will follow UNEP and GCF templates. Reports will be based on:

- Collection and consolidation of data as agreed in means of verification;
- Data used in the analysis will be available to external parties;
- Assessment of progress towards outputs will be based on a transparent process; In addition, and as needed, UNEP may conduct field visits to assess progress. GCF’s and Project Steering Committee members may also request to join these visits.


### Mid-term review/evaluation

The project will undergo an independent mid-term review/evaluation at year 3 of implementation. The midterm review/evaluation will assess progress towards outputs and identify issues requiring decisions and actions. The mid-term review/evaluation will provide feedback on project implementation and management.

### Terminal evaluation

An independent Terminal Evaluation (TE) will take place at the end of project implementation. UNEP's Evaluation Unit, which is responsible for undertaking the TE at the end of project implementation, which is a summative evaluation, and will base with the UNEP Task Manager throughout the process. An independent assessment team will be appointed by UNEP to conduct the evaluation.

The evaluation will assess the project’s effectiveness, efficiency, impact and sustainability. A weighted average will be determined to provide an overall performance rating for the project as a whole. The final evaluation report will be presented to the Project Steering Committee for approval. A final determination will be made by the Evaluation Office when the evaluation report is final.

The draft TE report will be sent to project stakeholders during a commencing process managed by the Evaluation Unit. This process will ensure that all stakeholders are informed of the evaluation in an open and transparent manner. This evaluation report will be publicly disclosed and will be followed by a recommendation compliance.

### F. Legal arrangements

#### 8.1. Legal basis for REDD-plus

Paraguay's legal framework does not define the ownership of carbon. However, the country has several laws and provisions that provide the necessary elements to implement REDD+. These include:

- **Law No. 156/00**: This law established the former SEM as an institutional body (now elevated to the rank of Ministry) with a mandate to manage forests in accordance with the principles of sustainable development, taking into consideration that the environment is a shared good of all citizens of Paraguay and with the aim of promoting a sustainable development model for this country. This mandate was strengthened with the creation of MADER.

- **Law No. 203/15**: This law establishes the role of the SEM as an environmental service provider in relation to climate change mitigation and to the sustainable use of the country's biological diversity. In order to achieve this, Law No. 203/15 also defines the rights and obligations between parties. It also defines the classification of types of agreements, the qualifications required for each party (public or private entities), the conditions that must be met by public entities (and private entities) and the necessary elements for its implementation. Law No. 203/15 gives MADER the mandate and responsibility to implement this law.

This regulation establishes that forest owners that meet the conditions to provide environmental services will be entitled to compensation for the services provided, in accordance with the guidelines established by the State. The SEM is responsible for implementing the law and establishing the necessary mechanisms for payment for environmental services. Forest owners do not have an automatic right to receive a payment for environmental services.

For example, it is not enough to preserve forest cover to receive payments. The area must also surpass a minimum threshold of 10 hectares and meet other requirements, among other elements, to comply with management requirements that will ensure the preservation of the forest.


---
**Page 82**
---

REDD-plus RFB FUNDING PROPOSAL GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 19 OF 83)

The case of MADES in implementing and managing funds for actions aimed at tackling climate change is also well defined by Law 5873 on Climate Change. As noted in article 2, paragraph 4, of the law, it is necessary to implement the actions that will reduce the country's vulnerability, improve its adaptive capacities and ensure sustainable development. The implementation of these actions must be carried out in accordance with the interpretation, its regulations and other administrative acts adopted as a consequence which will take into consideration the provisions of the Constitution, Law 5873 on Climate Change, the Declaration of Environment and Development adopted in Rio de Janeiro in 1992, the National Environmental Policy Act No. 40/96, and the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights. It is also important to note that the government of Paraguay will have to achieve in order to meet the obligations to which the country committed itself under international law.

Law 5873 creates the National Climate Change Fund whose objectives are to raise and implement public, private, national and international financial resources to support the implementation of actions to address climate change. The fund is also intended to ensure that the funds raised from these sources are used in a participatory manner with transparency and accountability. The law also establishes that contributions from international agencies and other national governments. The Law also allows for the use of public, private, national and international financial resources invested in order to undertake actions aimed at mitigating climate change.

Paraguay's current legal context, particularly Law 30156 on environmental services and Law 5873 on Climate Change, give MADES the mandate to receive and channel resources obtained from results-based payments. As for the implementation of these actions, it is necessary that the funds raised from these sources are invested in activities included in the ENBES in a participatory manner with transparency.

The support provided by the FCPF project, which is being managed in Paraguay by UNDP, includes actions to achieve the objectives set out above. The project is expected to be completed by 2025.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP121

## Proposal name: REED+ Results-based payments in Paraguay for the period 2015-2017

Accredited entity: United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

Country/ies): Paraguay

Project/programme size: Small

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

#### 1.1 Project background

The project presents Paraguay's REED+ plus results for the period 2015-2017, calculating a volume of around 23 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO₂e) in emission reductions derived from reducing emissions from deforestation, which have subsequently been converted into payments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), and which are undergoing technical assessment and have already been published in the UNFCCC's registry. The volume of 23 million tonnes of CO₂e is the total amount that has been declared, and thus the final volume presented to GCF for payments corresponds to 18,060,000 tCO₂e.

These results are presented to GCF for results-based payments (RBP) as part of the REED plus pilot project. The results have been assessed against the reference scenario set in the terms of reference (ToR) for the pilot programme (decision B18/07). The presented results, which include the volume of emission reductions and the scorecard, have been analyzed based on the scorecard and are presented in annex I to this assessment.

Paraguay will use the proceeds from the RBP to invest in the implementation of the National Climate Change Fund (ENCC), as well as other climate change projects consistent with its nationally determined contribution (NDC) and in the implementation of a National Climate Change Fund. The RBP project will focus on the following outputs:

(a) Output 1: Development of institutional arrangements and reporting systems put in place for the implementation of the ENCC

(b) Output 2: Governance and operational procedures of the Climate Change Fund established, and finance channeled to NGOs, the ENCC and low carbon development plans

#### 1.2 Scorecard results

As per decision B18/07, the proposal is currently being assessed using the scorecard for REED+ plus RBPs. This assessment is being conducted by the Secretariat with the support of Land Hay et al., 2016. The final score and associated comments will be discussed with the independent Technical Advisory Panel. The final score still needs to be discussed and agreed with the independent Technical Advisory Panel.

<sup>1</sup> Available at <https://redd.unfccc.int/>

### Strengths and points of caution

6. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:


---
**Page 92**
---

### Strengths

The National Strategy for Forests and Sustainable Growth (NIESG) is a strategic long-term commitment by the Government of Paraguay to reduce deforestation.

#### Points of clarification

The development of the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF) offers Paraguay a unique opportunity to make use of proceeds from REDD+ projects. The Board will need to make sure that a very innovative structure and setup is developed.

### Assessment of performance against the investment criteria

#### 2.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

Paraguay has implemented several policies and measures that contributed to the REDD+ results offered to GCF. These policies resulted in emission reductions of 26,793,111.02 tCO2e/year (45% of the total emissions) and avoided deforestation of 80,000 ha/year (4,140,000 ha/eq). These results were achieved by GCF resulting in 18,860,000 tCO2e. Paraguay has not reached its REDD+ targets for the period 2013-2015 and is still working on other sources. The total volume of emission reductions achieved by Paraguay in this period, including those proposed under the GCF, are included in the technical annex to the biennial update report submitted to the UNFCCC.

These results were technically assessed in accordance with the Conference of the Parties decision 1/CP.16 and the GCF Technical Assessment Framework (TAF) for aligning GCF objectives and targets in the forest and land-use results area, and furthermore, through the use of a technical assessment report prepared by an independent third party. The leveraging of finance to continue the Government's efforts in reducing deforestation. Due to the accreditation scope of the accredited entity, the proposal presents a GCF Volume of Emission Reductions (VER) of 18,860,000 tCO2e and corresponding emission reductions of USD 534 million.

#### 2.2 Paradigm shift potential
**Scale: High**

Paraguay has adopted the ambitious NIESG, which gives the Government the tools and infrastructure needed for forest restoration immediately and in the long term. In combination with other policies and measures, Paraguay achieved results from reducing


---
**Page 93**
---

emission, deforestation and can continue to do so with the strategic framework of the national strategy. ENCF builds on numerous national initiatives and strategies such as the national development plan 2030, national environmental policy, national climate change policy, national forest management plan, national forestry policy, agarian sector framework and the national energy policy.

The use of proceeds is innovative and strategic given the support to the implementation of ENCF. The project will be implemented in parallel with the National Climate Change Plan (NCCP) for the development of NCP to support the implementation and leverage additional finance. NCCP was established as a key component of Paraguay’s national climate change strategy. The use of proceeds will allow Paraguay to operationalize the fund in a very strategic and coherent manner. NCCF will support activities aligned with Paraguay’s NCCP and support the implementation of ENCF.

NCCF offers a unique opportunity for Paraguay to also leverage additional finance from the international community, including through the Green Climate Fund, to implement innovative approaches to reduce deforestation and create long-term transformational change. The creation of NCCF can be adapted to many other countries and regions and will serve as an important piloting of the use of proceeds for the creation of long-term national financial mechanisms.

### III. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 3.1 Environmental and social safeguards

28. The Government of Paraguay is submitting a proposal for the payment of the greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reductions generated by its REDD-plus activities for the period of 2015 to 2017. The two aspects related to the environmental and social safeguards considerations are addressed in the Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA). These were incorporated into this proposal. The first aspect entails an ex-post review of the extent to which the implementation of REDD-plus activities has been consistent with the requirements of the safeguards framework for REDD-plus agreed upon under the UNFCCC, also referred to as Cancun Safeguards, and the GCF’s accredited entity (AE) environmental and social safeguards and relevant policies. The second aspect is an ex-ante review of the consistency between the assessment of the environmental and social risks and impacts of activities supported by the payment for results compliant with the requirements of the environmental and social safeguards related policies of the AE.

30. In relation to the first aspect, the AE prepared an Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) that describes how the REDD-plus activities were developed and implemented in ways that are consistent with the Cancun safeguards and the safeguards frameworks of GCF and AES. The ESA describes the activities that were developed and implemented that have enabled the generation of GHG emission reductions for the period 2015 to 2017.

31. The development and implementation of REDD-plus activities require, among others, that the activities are consistent with relevant policies, laws, regulations, and other requirements (TOR). This entailed interpreting the Cancun safeguards according to the country’s context. The interpretation of the Cancun Safeguards was documented in the ESA. The description of how the assessment of how each safeguard was addressed and respected throughout the implementation of REDD-plus activities is contained in the ESA. The description of how the safeguards were addressed and respected is contained in the country's Summary of Information (SOI). The information on the safeguards that are contained in the SOI and includes the compatibility with the REDD-plus safeguards with the country’s policies, laws, regulations, consistency of its relevant policies, laws, and regulations with the AE’s environmental, social and economic screening.

31. Complementing the ESA, the AE has also developed an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) that describes the due diligence processes that will govern how the REDD-plus activities are implemented in a manner consistent with addressing both the environmental and social risks and impacts associated with such activities. The risks and impacts of REDD-plus activities are assessed as part of the AE’s screening process, which is captured in the Environmental, Social and Economic Review Note (ESERN). The ESERN is prepared for each project or activity that is proposed to be located at a specific location. The ESERNs have yet to be finalized; the ESMF provides the process for screening, sourcing, further assessment and management of risks and impacts associated with the activities once determined.

#### 3.1.1 Consistency of the design and implementation of REDD-plus activities with the Cancun safeguards and the GCF AE ESF

36. The ESA of the REDD-plus results-based mechanism in Paraguay for the period 2015–2017 is consistent with the requirements of the Cancun safeguards and considers the country’s legal framework, activities and results of risk analysers and management; how stakeholders

The ESA concludes that, based on elements reported to the assessment team, results obtained from reducing deforestation were consistent with the Cancun safeguards and the AE’s environmental and social objectives. The ESA notes that the Paraguayan government has implemented a range of policies, actions, and measures that “considered criteria for risk mitigation and/or avoided adverse impacts” in line with the National REDD+ Strategy, which are relevant to determining consistency and application of the required safeguards. These include:

- Strengthening respect for indigenous rights, resources, and cultures.
- Concrete actions were taken to address risks associated with indigenous peoples and issues that may arise from REDD+ implementation.
- The Paraguayan government has developed a National Action Plan (NAP) – consistent with GCF safeguard requirements – that indigenous peoples and their representatives should be involved in decision-making on implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy, including through the establishment of appropriate mechanisms for resource distribution and REDD-plus benefits.

A need was identified to strengthen respect for community property. The ESA recommends that land use planning and the application of conflict resolution mechanisms – both consistent with GCF safeguards and policies and strategies of the AE – be strengthened in this regard as well as one

Strengthening the application of Cancun safeguards on the risk of reversals of emissions reductions achieved through REDD+ activities, including those related to conservation for landowners affected by prohibitions set out in Paraguayan law. As the ESA notes, the objective of Paraguayan Law 2524 (the “Zero Deforestation Law”) is to ensure that no new deforestation occurs and that existing forests are protected.

It is important to recognize that although the risks assessments involving various activities on the REDD-plus strategies undertaken in years 2013-2014 are not part of the period identified as covered by the results-based proposal, they were an integral part of the activities developed before 2015 and, accordingly, as described in the ESA, served as a basis for constraining the design of the REDD-plus strategies. The same applies to the actions implemented in the period 2015-2017. From 2015 onwards, risk identification focused on reducing deforestation through the implementation of the National Forest Strategy (Estrategia Nacional Bosques para el Crecimiento Sostenible or ENB) across greater parishes.

The ESA states that, consistent with GCF and Cancun safeguard requirements, information was provided to stakeholders in a timely manner. The process involved close engagement of relevant stakeholders, and that various processes, requirements, and tools were established for the development of operational plans and stakeholder participation programs. It describes Paraguay’s approach of “participatory stakeholder participation processes” for engaging with a broad and inclusive range of stakeholders. The years or period of development of these plans, as part of the National REDD+ Strategy, are not specified in the results-based proposal. The MADRS led the consultation process for the elaboration of the results-based proposal covering the year 2015 to 2017. Following on those consultation processes, the authorities

The ESA concludes that the results were achieved through the country's policies and measures that were determined to be consistent with its AE's safeguards as well as the Canarian safeguard standards. ### Environmental and social due diligence for the use of payment for results

The EMF was developed to support the due-diligence of activities implemented under the national REDD+ strategy "Estrategia Nacional Bosques por el Cambio Climático Sostenible (ENBCCS)". The ENDCS is a strategic document that sets out the actions and measures required by the results-based payment. These two components: implementation of several concrete actions of ENDCS that will address the drivers of deforestation, and supporting operational activities to be carried out under the National Climate Change Act 2018 (NCCCA) with the aim of channelling financial resources to support the climate change adaptation and mitigation measures in order to generate beneficial results. However, it is anticipated that the activities may have adverse environmental or social risks.

The AE considers the environmental and social risks associated with the activities that will be supported by the results-based payments as moderate, equivalent to Category B under the IGF framework. The EMF provides information on the expected environmental and social risks and impacts of the activities implemented in the ENDCS. The ESA also provides information about the measures regarding the anticipated risks and the mitigation measures that can be designed and implemented to manage the identified risks.

The preliminary screening identified key environmental and social risks and impacts which include, among others, potential adverse impacts on biodiversity, displacement of


---
**Page 98**
---

deforestación en otras áreas, impactos sobre los derechos de las personas indígenas y posibles restricciones al acceso a tierras y recursos naturales que pueden llevar a la pérdida de viviendas de afectadas comunidades. Las siguientes actividades identifican riesgos preliminares ambientales y sociales descritos a continuación:

### 1. Desplazamiento involuntario
Las actividades que pueden conducir a desplazamientos involuntarios no estarán soportados en la implementación del REBIO más el marco de país. El uso de procedimientos de compensación para restablecer las viviendas y los medios de vida de las personas afectadas por el proyecto solo abordará arreglos de tenencia de tierras. Aunque la mitigación será la medida clave, se realizará un análisis de riesgos y impactos ambientales y sociales en el marco del procedimiento de evaluación ambiental para el proyecto. Donde haya la posibilidad de restricción de acceso a reasentamiento, el ABF o sus entidades asociadas desarrollará un Plan de Acción de Desarrollo de Viviendas y Medios de Vida (DAP) en colaboración con las comunidades afectadas y se implementará en el marco del Fondo ECO.

### 2. Restricción de acceso a tierras o uso de recursos para la supervivencia
Las actividades que pueden conducir a restricciones de acceso a tierras y usos de tierras para la supervivencia incluyen la limitación de prácticas tradicionales y de vida de subsistencia, el reconocimiento de derechos de uso de tierras por parte de las personas indígenas y pequeños agricultores. Las compensaciones se realizarán para pérdidas de activos o acceso a recursos naturales que no estén cubiertos por la compensación. Los alternativos serán evaluados e identificados para restablecer los medios de vida de las comunidades afectadas.

### 3. Pueblos indígenas
Algunas de las actividades pueden tener impactos adversos sobre los pueblos indígenas, incluyendo la limitación del acceso a tierras bajo sistemas tradicionales, pérdida de medios de vida y medidas para apoyar el uso sostenible de recursos naturales. Tales impactos adversos podrían llevar a la degradación de los sistemas tradicionales de conocimiento y herencia. tales impactos adversos podrían llevar a la degradación de los sistemas tradicionales de conocimiento y herencia. Tal impacto adverso podría llevar a la degradación de los sistemas tradicionales de conocimiento y herencia.

### 4. Gestión del cambio climático
Las actividades que se enfocarán en mejorar el potencial de desarrollo sostenible, como el uso de tierras para planificación de uso, legalización de áreas protegidas, mejoras relacionadas con la agricultura y sistemas agrícolas, implementación de políticas de conservación del suelo, mejora de prácticas agrícolas sostenibles, expansión de la agricultura y ganadería en el marco de las políticas nacionales de INIFONA y MADES, implementación de un sistema forestal de monitoreo y desarrollo de sistemas de alerta temprana.

### 5. Riesgos derivados de retroalimentaciones y emisiones
Los descriptores del ESMI describen riesgos provenientes de la no persistencia de reducciones de emisiones y disminución de deforestación en otras áreas que podrían afectar a las actividades del proyecto. Se implementará un plan de mitigación de riesgos ambientales, sociales y económicos en el marco del procedimiento de evaluación ambiental social y económica sostenible. El análisis preliminar de riesgos se realizará para identificar posibles riesgos ambientales, sociales y económicos.

### Further Environmental and Social Assessment

The corresponding management plans will be undertaken during the project’s inception process. As may be determined in the detailed environmental and social assessments, the ESMS will be updated, and the safeguards plans will be prepared. These include a land use plan, an environmental and social monitoring and reporting plan, a stakeholder engagement plan, among others. The outlines of such plans are included in the ESMS.

#### Activities

- A general eligibility criterion is included in the ESMS: As a general eligibility criterion, only activities considered as having none to low environmental and social risks will be supported.
- Activities that may lead to significant adverse impacts on the environment or human rights will not be supported. In addition, activities that involve involuntary resettlement will not be supported by the results-based payment funds.

Other excluded activities include those that may lead to violations of international law, including indigenous peoples’ rights, such as land owned, or claimed under adjudication, by indigenous peoples, without full documented consent by the indigenous communities concerned. The ESMS also includes a list of excluded activities.

#### Stakeholder Engagement

The AE discussed in the ESMS the stakeholder engagement process that went into the development of the results-based payment proposal. The documentation of the stakeholder consultation and engagement process is extensive and detailed, and builds on the extensive stakeholder engagement conducted through the REDD+ pilot readiness phase supported by the GCF. This includes a wide range of stakeholders from government bodies such as the MADES and Secretariat of Planning for Economic and Social Development (NIUE), the Institute of Indigenous Peoples’ Rights (INIDEI), the National Commission for Self-Determination of Indigenous Peoples (Federación por la Autodeterminación de los Pueblos Indígenas) (FAPI), indigenous peoples’ organizations, local communities, civil society, and non-governmental organizations and cooperatives. During the implementation, the AE commits to continue strengthening the link of the project with the indigenous peoples community and to support their capacity building in order to ensure that they are able to monitor that the governance structure of the ENICS, aligned with the Cancun safeguards and the AR and GCF safeguard policies, is being respected.

The stakeholder engagement plan will be developed during the project inception phase that will identify the relevant stakeholders for each component of the project. The AE commits to engage all stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities, activity level engagement roles, and mechanisms for engaging with them throughout the project implementation period.

#### Stakeholder Response Mechanism

The ESMS described the AE’s Stakeholder Response Mechanism and its processes as applied to the project. The mechanism covers the processes for receiving and resolving complaints from stakeholders in a fair, transparent, and timely manner, and in an open and transparent manner. This Stakeholder Response Mechanism, along with the existing complaints handling mechanisms of the ENICS, will be used by the AE to ensure that all complaints are addressed in a manner that will be accessible to all stakeholders and aligned to the principles of confidentiality, culturally appropriate, transparent and inclusive. The mechanism for developing a project-level grievance response mechanism is described in the ESMS; for example, the grievance mechanisms, the UN Environment’s stakeholder response mechanism, ENICS-developed mechanisms, and the AE’s own grievance response mechanism are all described.

The ESMS also describes how the AE will further develop its grievance response redress mechanism will be further defined, specifying procedures that include receiving and registering complaints, investigating complaints, resolving complaints, communicating about the facilitation and mechanisms for accessing particularly for indigenous peoples and local communities. The UN Environment’s stakeholder response mechanism will be used as a temporary solution for the project.

The ESMS included the overall strategy for assessing the performance of environmental and social safeguards in the project. This includes an overview of how the AE plans the conduct of mid-term evaluation and review, and compliance reviews. In terms of

3.2 Gender policy

The All has submitted a gender assessment; therefore, it complies with the operational guidelines of the Gender Policy and Action Plan. The All has also provided a gender action plan.

The assessment indicates that Paraguay is signatory to several international conventions that focus on gender equality and women's empowerment. The national constitution guarantees equality of rights and non-discrimination between women and men. Paraguay has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, among others. The National Gender Strategy on Climate Change (ENCC) is a key policy document that sets out the government's commitment to ensuring equal opportunities and engagement of women and men in development in the country, including the REDD+ interventions.

The assessment also provides information on the state of inequality between women and men in Paraguay, where inequalities are seen in the following areas: education, particularly in terms of access to quality education; unequal distribution of domestic and care work of women, which restricts their ability to participate in the formal labor force; limited participation of women in political decision-making; and women's limited access to land. Land is a key factor that determines the livelihoods of the rural population. Women rarely have 33 per cent of forest land and are not involved in the management or decision making regarding forests. The sector lacks gender equality consideration, affecting women's participation in forest governance and their ability to benefit from economic opportunities related to the forest and forest-related resources. The assessment also identifies key needs and priorities for both women and men in relation to REDD+ and forest conservation, including access to economic opportunities and benefits sharing. While these are important issues for both men and women, they are particularly relevant for women who are often excluded by lack of access to natural resources and economic opportunities and benefits. These issues were extracted from the stakeholder consultations that were conducted.

The assessment also highlights the work done in various consultancies, as well as the women's ministry and civil society groups that work on women's issues. The assessment also highlighted the issues identified through the consultations with indigenous people and other vulnerable groups.


---
**Page 101**

The elaboration and implementation of the indigenous peoples plan as well as the stakeholder engagement plan will also explore opportunities to address observed gender gaps. Raising awareness on gender issues is one other critical intervention that needs to be addressed in the action plan. The AE and EE are expected to provide support for the implementation and monitoring of activities in the action plan.

54. The AE is advised to provide clear plans on how the BRFs will be administered given the existing guidelines and policies, including those related to land use planning, forest management, and planning for protected areas and land titling in addition to the existing underlying project activities. These plans should also reflect tangible actions that reflect the needs and priorities of women; it should also be able to contribute to enhanced participation and benefits from project investment for women.

### 3.3 Risks

#### 3.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium):

57. Under the pilot programme for REDD-plus BRF, GCF is requested to provide a payment of USD 8 million for the emission reductions achieved by Paraguay in the period 2015-2017. This funding will be provided as part of the overall project financing package. The deadline for which GCF funding is sought, is within 30 per cent of the total payable volume, as required under the TDR.

58. The end-use activities are in accordance with the national REDD-plus action plan and as permitted under the TDR. The AE and EE are expected to coordinate with various stakeholders for the implementation of the end-use activities.

#### 3.3.2 Accredited entity/exceeding entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk):

59. The United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) is accredited for a small proportion (up-to USD 50 million). The AE has a track record in the implementation of projects, including REDD-plus.

60. As mentioned in the funding proposal, the current capacity of MABIP as the EE to ensure the timely and effective implementation of the project is limited by several factors. However, the project implementation depends on actions of multiple stakeholders, ranging from different national ministries and local governments. GCF is relying on the AE to ensure proper coordination with these stakeholders in order to ensure the use of the disbursements.

#### 3.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk):

61. Risk of double payments: The funding proposal indicates that the Government is in the process of establishing a national registry to track emission reductions achieved. A first version of the registry has been developed and tested, but it is not yet operational. Emission reductions for which the BRF is made will not be transferred and/or used for other payments in the future. The funding proposal also indicates that there will be no transactions except for the proposed BRF until the registry is established and made publicly available.

62. Impact of end-use activities: The AE uses the proceeds from BRFs to support the implementation of ENBIs and the operationalization of NCFF. Thus, the end use as permitted in the TDR, the proposed activities will be implemented over six years and then need to be adequately monitored. As the end-use activities will be implemented over six years, it is


---
**Page 102**
---

recommended that the disbursement of GCF financing be carried out over the six-year period corresponding with the implementation of the activities.

### Structure of NCF

NCF has been enhanced in line and RFPs will be used to operationalize the fund. The expected size of NCF after the capitalization of RPB is about USD 18,602 million. The structure of NCF will be similar to that of GCF but with some activities in line with NCDS, ENCS and low-carbon development, with low or moderate risk projects. However, the detailed structure of NCF has not yet been finalized, while the structure and operation of the fund need to be carefully set up.

#### Competing claim for the carbon ownership

The funding proposal states that Paraguay will receive 100% of the GCF volume of emission reductions from the country since the country has several laws and precedents that provide MADES (the EE) with the mandate to receive, manage and sell the carbon credits generated by the projects. The GCF will be used for forest protection. The AE will ensure that (1) the GCF volume of emission reductions and the RPB are not eligible for GCF under any arrangement; (2) the emission reductions paid off by GCF are no longer eligible for RPB under other arrangements; and (3) the emission reductions will be retired and will not be transferred or used again.

##### Compliance risk (medium risk)

The AE has provided a detailed description of its program to address, monitor and mitigate compliance risks related to avoid financing and prohibited practices.

Within the funding proposal itself, the AE explicitly outlines the money-laundering/terrorist-financing risk mitigation strategy through downstream contractual obligations. The AE also states that it will enter into an anti-money laundering/corruption master agreement (AMA) with UNDP, clause 9-03. UNDP shall apply its own fiduciary principles with regard to the use of funds and ensure that the AMA is applied in all transactions with its customer checks, among others. However, in order to effectively address the risks arising from origin LAC countries, a risk mitigation strategy for these countries will be developed as an essential part of the organizational set-up of NCF-PEA. A combination of these measures is expected to reduce the overall risk exposure to a medium level.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this project on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-material and therefore, no further assessment of concentration levels results area or single proposal.

#### Conclusion

It is recommended that any approval by the Board be made by considering the above points.

## 3.4 Results monitoring and reporting

Following the requirements of the TOR, the proposal will follow simplified reporting for which a specific template was developed by the Secretariat. Reporting will be delivered annually.

Monitoring of the use of proceeds will be conducted at the main activity level according to the description provided in the funding proposal.

## 3.5 Legal assessment

The AMA was signed with the AE on 15 December 2016 and became effective on 20 February 2017.

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all necessary approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in Paraguay, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against expropriation or nationalization of its assets. The project will further assess. The Secretariat submitted the first draft of the privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of Paraguay on 15 December 2016 and updated it on 3 April 2018. The draft agreement is currently in negotiation.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independence Integrity Unit have both reviewed the project documentation and confirmed that they will pursue their relevant activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. It should be noted that the GCF are only protected against expropriation if the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection through litigation and expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

## IV. List of proposed conditions

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following:

Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the Date of Board approval; and

Completion of the relevant due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.
