Based on this information, the AT considers that the pools' litter and deadwood are unlikely to be significant pools relative to other land uses. Therefore, their omission from the FREL is justified. However, the AT considers that Paraguay should provide additional information provided to justify the omission of these pools.

Regarding soils, the AT considers that, depending on the magnitude of soil carbon stock changes when reforestation takes place, this pool might be a significant source of emissions as there is a large quantity of carbon stored in these soils. The AT proposes that the inclusion of the change in the amount of soil in other land uses prevents a proper estimation of the emission from this pool. It also considers that including the carbon content of soil would overestimate the emissions from this pool. The AT therefore considers that the omission of this pool is justified, but it should be noted that further studies have noting the magnitude of the preliminary results on soil carbon stock changes in Paraguay and the assessment of the significance of emissions from this pool as an area of dereliction should be considered once when the necessary information becomes available.

(vi) Actions to address the risks of reversals.

The ENBCS and other national laws list the various actions to help address the risks of reversals such as intensifying the production of agriculture to reduce expansion into forest areas, environmental licensing, and the use of a land-use change monitoring and information system. In particular, ENBCS focuses on enhancing sustainable management practices and foreseid practices to help reduce the need to expand the agricultural frontier.

In addition, a buffer has been established to further diminish the risk of reversals, estimated based on the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) methodology. The National Climate Change Policy outlines the use of the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility's methods for calculating carbon stocks and emissions.

(vii) Actions to reduce displacement of emissions.

The ENBCS has embedded several measures to help reduce displacement of emissions such as potential impacts of livelihoods, improved land-use planning, and the use of forest-based products. Event control and monitoring systems to detect and report displacement.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP121

## Proposal name:
REDD+ Results-based payments in Paraguay for the period 2015-2017

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

## Project/programme size:
Small

### I. Scorecard application
The Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP), with the support of a Land Use, Land Change and Forests (LULUCF) expert selected from the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) roster of experts and with experience in REDD-plus accounting methodologies, has assessed the proposal for results-based payments for the period 2015-2017 using the scorecard included in the terms of reference for the REDD-plus results-based payments (RBP) pilot programme.

#### 1.1 Summary of scorecard results

| Scorecard section | Results |
| --- | --- |
| Carbon elements | Score: 36 All criteria "pass" |
| Non-carbon elements | Canopy safeguards All criteria "pass" |
| Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits | Score: 2 |

#### 2. Based on the above results and following the terms of reference procedures, the ITAP recommends that the Board consider the following:
(a) GCF volume of emission reductions: 14,145,000 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO₂eq).
(b) Additional 2.5 per cent for use of proceeds and non-carbon elements; and
(c) Proposed REDD plus RBP USD 5/tCO₂eq: USD 72,493,125.
(d) Due to the accreditation scope of the accredited entity the proposal presents the first 50 million USD which corresponds to a GCF volume of emission reductions: 9,756,098 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO₂eq). The remaining results-based payments and corresponding emission reductions will be presented in a subsequent proposal by Paraguay and the accredited entity.

### II. Main findings


---
**Page 121**
---

### B1.(q) Consistency between the Forest Reference Emission Levels (FREL)/Forest Reference Level (FRL/FRL) and the greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory: the Assessment Team (AT) noted that the information on GHG emissions provided in the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project document is not fully consistent with the information used in the construction of the FREL, in particular for the year 2015. The AT also noted that the methodology and assumptions used by Paraguay to derive the FREL are different from those used to derive the GHG inventory.

### B1.(r) Accuracy of the FREL/FRL: some issues were raised during the UNFCCC FREL Technical Assessment (TA) that negatively affect the accuracy of Paraguay's FREL regarding the treatment of palm areas as an independent forest stratum for activity data and emissions factors. The AT noted that these issues are related to the methodology, and the development of specific allometric equations for the estimation of total and above-ground biomass in palm areas. The AT also noted that the issues were raised by the UNFCCC FREL TA. The AT acknowledged the intention expressed by Paraguay to continuously improve the collection of data on palm areas as part of its ongoing efforts towards these goals, as part of the stepwise approach, for future FREL submissions (paragraph 44(b) of the technical guidance). The AT also noted that the issues raised are related to ongoing undelineed work and a proposed improvement plan that will address the identified issues. The score is: 1 significant issues were raised and were resolved; or issues were raised but not resolved, or the issues are considered to be material to the accuracy of the FREL/FRL, and the country has provided a plan on how it will resolve them.

### B1.(s) Inclusion of the most significant pools: the AT acknowledged that Paraguay included in the FREL the most significant activity and the most significant pools in terms of emissions. The AT also noted that Paraguay used the results from the estimation of soil organic carbon in mineral soils, Paraguay provided preliminary results of carbon content in palm areas, which were not used to derive the FREL. The carbon content estimated for a depth of 50 cm ranges from 13.87 t/ha to 24.96 t/ha and was derived using soil samples collected at different depths. Paraguay explained that these preliminary results cannot be used for the estimation of CO2 emissions from this pool owing to the lack of information on carbon content of soil for other land-use categories, which is needed to assess the net variation of carbon


---
**Page 122**
---

resulting from deforestation. The AT commended Paraguay for its efforts in the collection and provision of information on this carbon pool, and agreed that the lack of information on carbon content of soil on other land uses prevents a proper estimation of the emissions from this pool. The AT therefore considered that the conclusions drawn by Paraguay were premature and should be revised after considering the magnitude of the preliminary results on soil carbon stock provided by Paraguay, considering the potential for further improvement. Likewise, the AT considered that, depending on the magnitude of the organic soils in which deforestation takes place, this pool might be a significant source of emissions from land use change. The AT noted that, based on its analysis, therefore, the assessment of the significance of emissions from this pool was seen as an area for further improvement. In relation to the carbon stock in forest biomass, deadwood and litter, the AT considered that they are probably not significant relative to other sources of emissions from land use change. The AT noted that, based on its analysis, however, the AT noted that Paraguay should enhance the information provided to justify the numerical values used for the estimation of emissions from this pool. In relation to the FREL, the AT acknowledged the interest of Paraguay to continuously improve the collection of information on activity data, emission factors and emitted carbon pools. Moreover, the findings of the analysis of the preliminary results in the context of the TAFR have been launched that is assumed to address these issues. The score is 1: "no, but justified due to lack of information", as the AT considered that further improvements are needed for forest removals, noting that countries should indicate a plan to include data in the future".

### B.1.(q) Inclusion of all significant sources of emissions: The AT considered that the country has included all significant sources of emissions from land use change into the FREL. Nevertheless, the AT identified the treatment of emissions of non-CO2 and, specifically, methane (CH4) as an area for further improvement. This is in line with technical improvement [paragraph 43 (c) of the TAFR]. The Paraguay's funding proposal points out that this issue will be addressed by including a new activity data set on CH4 emissions when analysing the significance of emissions of gases emitted from the FREL. The corresponding score is therefore: "1: no, but justified due to lack of information". This assessment does not overestimate emissions, noting that countries should indicate a plan to include data in the future".

### B.1.(r) Compliance with previous FREL/Paraguay's FREL is the first reference level submitted to the UNFCCC in the context of RIL. The corresponding score is 1: "no adjustment needed", as Paraguay has indicated that it will not make any adjustments.

## Paraguay: A Case Study

The Paraguay River divides the country into two distinct zones: those of the west, which are generally drier and more arid, and those of the east which are more humid and include the Gran Chaco region and the Bosque Atlántico. The Gran Chaco which is shared by Argentina, Bolivia, Brazil and Paraguay has been designated as a UNESCO World Heritage Site.

The Paraguayan Chaco lost 14,640,441 hectares from 2001 to 2014 (World Resources Institute), largely due to the expansion of agriculture. The Bosque Atlántico is an important Atlantic Forest Ecoregion, once present in Argentina and Brazil and recognized as one of the world's most endangered ecosystems with only about 5% of its original cover.

Land tenure has been historically driven by inequitable land assignments and acquisitions: 85 per cent of the land in Paraguay is in the hands of 2.5 per cent of the population (Dilman, 1997).

Paraguay reduced emissions from deforestation at the national level in the order of 26,793,311 tCO2e during the period 2015-2017(32,020,721 tCO2e/year for 2015–2016 and 32,778,400 tCO2e/year for 2017) through the implementation of actions and measures that took place in that period. The LULUCF sector has historically been the main source of deforestation.

Paraguay completed its first BUR in 2015, including the preparation of the national GHG inventory for the LULUCF sector, and later presented BUR 2 in 2018. The Second Biennial Update Report (BUR) was submitted to UNFCCC on 3 April 2020, with a total net rate of 31 per cent, behind those generated by the agricultural sector, estimated at 53 per cent.

Paraguay submitted its first FRL on a voluntary basis on 4 January 2016, and later a revised version in June 2017. The FRL was developed with the support of the Ministry of Environment from the technical assessment to improve the transparency and consistency of the FRL. The development of the FRL is based on the results of the LULUCF inventory carried out for a 15-year period (2000–2015), and covers the entire Paraguayan territory.

The LULUCF sector concluded that the following areas for technical improvement identified in the first FRL are still relevant: (a) the need to apply the same methodology also apply to the provision of information on the results of implementation of the activity reducing emissions from deforestation; (b) the need to improve the consistency between the LULUCF data and the definition used by the GHG inventory for the calculation of the emission factor; (c) the need to define to ensure consistency across official submissions and in its practical implementation when assessing the impact of land use change on emissions from deforestation, including deforestation to improve consistency with the GHG inventory and to avoid overestimation of emission reductions associated with the activity reducing emissions from deforestation; (d) the need for a more detailed description of the methodology used to estimate the potential soil carbon stocks after forest conversion in order to assess the significance, in terms of emissions, of these post-2015 activities. The FRL also includes an explanation on how to distinguish sub-site to deliberation or to justify omission of the emissions on the basis of their insignificance and/or irrelevance for the country, specifically those resulting from the practice of slash and burn; (g) improvement of consistency in terms of plots and gases included with the

# National GHG Inventory

Paraguay is making continuous efforts to enhance the information and data in accordance with the recommendations.

## The methodologies provided for calculating emission reductions are clear. Paraguay asked for support of $230,600,000 (CO2-eq 2015–2016-2017) that corresponds to approximately 48% of the total of the first generation of emission reductions programme and has a plan to ask for $7 million from the next generation of emission reductions, summing $30,600,000 (CO2-eq 2015–2016-2017). The methodology used in this calculation a buffer of 10 per cent is included. A 58.763,935 t CO2-eq was confirmed for Paraguay for the period 2015–2016.

## The use of the proceeds needs better refinement to ensure effective future emission reductions from deforestation. The proposal is to use the proceeds to enable implementation of the Emission Reductions Fund (ERF) under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Programme for Sustainable Growth), which is a guiding document. The ENBICS includes a total of 41 actions (including 20 in the area of forest management and 21 in the area of land use change). The proposal is to use around 58 per cent of the proceeds to enable these actions with very broad criteria for eligibility, which could lead to an increase in deforestation. It also sets out the rules of operation of a national climate fund and its governance and to capitalize the Fund with US$17.4 million. The proposal presents an annex with a very basic definition of this fund, stating that it will be used to finance actions aimed at reducing deforestation and meet the country’s nationally determined contribution and the ENBICS, and that specific eligibility criteria for projects will be defined in the future.

## REPs provide Paraguay with a major opportunity in the fight against deforestation. However, REPs alone cannot carry the whole financing weight nor can they substitute for decisive action by the Government of Paraguay to address the root causes of deforestation through a means to achieve a result. As presented in this assessment, the Government of Paraguay could capitalize the Emission Reductions Fund (ERF) with US$17.4 million and use it to reduce further deforestation and ensure long-term sustainability of the fund with a real vision of change. The impact would be significant if the Government of Paraguay were to focus on the more use of the proceeds, blending finance to stop deforestation and to promote regeneration of critical ecosystems such as the Selva Atlantica and the Gran Chaco.

## 2.2 Paradigm shift potential

Paraguay is a small country with important ecosystems such as the Gran Chaco and Mata Atlantica. However, over time, due to years of mismanagement, deforestation for cattle ranching and soya production has increased, almost reaching saturation take place from 2013. From 2013, deforestation started to show a decline, which was accentuated in the 2015–2017 period. However, this trend is not sustainable as it is based on the assumption that the law closing the forestry law to enable deforestation of forest reserves, with significant negative effects on land use and rural development. The Government of Paraguay should consider revising or re-closing the window for further deforestation. Even though preliminary research shows that deforestation has been reduced in the period 2015–2017, it is still early to claim positive results. As shown in the following table, the deforestation rate of 2017 returned to the levels of 2010.

## Changing the practices to stop deforestation will require strong commitments from the Government and/or private sector, with laws that remain in place independently of the change in government, and/or a legal framework.

### The proceeds will be used for implementation of the ENSICS, including a list of actions intended to address and reduce indirect causes of forest loss: barriers to change and legal/regulatory frameworks.

#### 10. The priority actions include:
- Strengthening of the Protected Areas System (both public and private sectors)
- Legal framework, including the application of penalties
- Full implementation of Law 3801 on Payment for Environmental Services (PES) and the Forest Information System (monitoring system)
- Support for implementing land-use planning
- Implementation of the Environment and Social Management Framework; strengthening of environmental governance at all levels through capacity building and training, and creation of sustainable economic alternatives for local actors and knowledge management and knowledge-sharing.

#### 10. The proceeds will also be used to capitalize the national climate change fund for actions that will ensure carbon and non-carbon benefits. However, the definition of the fund and innovative financing mechanisms are still under discussion. It is expected that this RLP from this fund will give a signal to the Government that protecting the environment pays off, implying that it is possible to generate economic returns through PES.

#### 10. The proceeds will also be used for the promotion of sustainable land use and expansion of agricultural frontiers while ensuring binding finance from public and private sectors such as agribusinesses, financial institutions, and international organizations to promote regenerative agriculture. The Paraguayan economy will need to respond to the world markets in order to ensure that it is not left behind.

#### 10. The provision of innovative and adapted incentives is a critical tool for forest conservation. The ITAP notes that the proposal includes support to the full implementation of Law 3801 on PES, which is currently under discussion with the Government. This is particularly because transactions happen between private actors with deficits and surpluses in the legally required areas of environmental services.

#### 10. The success of this RLP will depend on the sustainability of the system of payments for environmental services is fully dependent on the enforcement of the forestry law, which in turn requires strong political support.

#### 10. The participation process has been a key component of the ENSICS involving project partners Institute for Environmental Research and Development (IREDE), the National Council for Autodeterminación de los Pueblos Indígenas (Federation for the self-determination of Indigenous Peoples), the National Institute of Biodiversity (INBio) and key actors in the public sector, non-governmental organizations, civil society, and private sector. The ENSICS has been designed to ensure that it is possible to have effective participation from indigenous peoples and smaller farmers (campesinos). The strategy was validated in 2010 through a process that involved participation of 306 people.

which is per cent were women and 57 per cent were men. The REDD-plus Working Group created within the framework of the National Commission on Climate Change, is the formal platform for discussion and technical consultations on REDD-plus.

However, these consultations should be undertaken with more active participation and ownership by ENBRS actors. Stakeholder meetings could involve private sector actors as well as the main actors involved in deforestation as well as indigenous communities and smallholder farmers. The objective of such meetings would be to ensure that all stakeholders are aware of those delivering REDD-plus results, with a mechanism to provide economic and financial incentives for the delivery of measurable, quantifiable or verifiable actions that are delivering concrete REDD-plus results.

The project presents a comprehensive action plan which presents the situation of resources in Paraguay and the six elements that will be considered as part of programming in the design and implementation of the proposed activities. These include: (1) strengthening accountability for forest management, including through the development of indicators for gender equity using indicators and protocols for evaluation; (3) establishing oversight through monitoring and evaluation systems to ensure the sustainable use of natural and biological resources; (5) capacity-building for gender mainstreaming; and (6) ensuring coherence knowledge and information management. However, the proposed states that further engagement is required at this stage of design, planning and designing actions ensuring that gender issues are mainstreamed.


## Sustainable development potential

The historic and contemporary rates of deforestation in Paraguay suggest that environmental conservation is secondary to an economic growth imperative based on agriculture and energy production. The country's dependence on the export of soybeans and its importance of standing forests for the provision of ecosystem services at large. Preserving the forests in Paraguay is therefore critical not only for the preservation of biodiversity but also for conserving a vast repository of biological diversity contributing to the Aichi Biodiversity Targets.

Paraguay depends on the export of energy from its Itaipu hydroelectric dam that in turn depends on the stability of ecosystem services provided by forests. It is imperative for Paraguay to understand and manage these trade-offs between economic development and conservation of ecosystems that interact to maintain water cycles in Latin American countries, including the Amazon Basin. The most important forested areas are located in the departments of Alto Paraná and Cainguas (where Itaipu is located) lost 237,900 hectares of forest between 2004 (when the zero deforestation law was first issued) and 2017. A World Resources Institute study estimated that the annual cost of deforestation in Paraguay from deforestation actions costs USD 23 million annually. Forests are therefore critical to ensure economic development, water supply and soil conservation.

The benefits of standing forests are also crucial to regulate rain and water provision for consumption and agriculture. Ensuring water for agriculture is only possible if the ecosystem services provided by standing forests are preserved. The preservation of these services contributes to soil erosion and stabilization.


## In terms of social benefits

In terms of social benefits, the use of the proceeds should ensure more direct incentives or disincentives for landholders who drive deforestation. Since 85 per cent of the land in Paraguay is in the hands of 2.5 per cent of the population, the use of resources that will be generated from REDD-plus could provide a mechanism to reduce inequality. The design of payments for results might not change the business as usual model of arbitrariness. Rather, the use of the proceeds could be used better to analyze the current financial and economic

models for land finding deforestation On the other hand, the use of the proceeds should also provide equitable and efficient schemes for peasants and indigenous communities that live in only 6.3 per cent of the agricultural land. They could use the RUP to pilot regeneration schemes with restitution of forest areas.

21. In addition, several efforts have been made to support land titling, including the work of the National Institute for Rural and Land Development on a land cadaster and titling of land that followed the 2014 constitutional reform. The Paraguayan government has also provided support to the land titling process. Complementary to this, the Paraguayan Indigenous Institute provided training in land rights and land titling to indigenous communities to help them understand and promote respect for the land rights of these peoples. Land titling will greatly support the livelihoods of peasants and indigenous communities.

24. Forest fires are also increasing, mostly in the Western Region. According to INFONA, about 60,000 hectares were affected during August and September 2019. Other fire areas have been spotted in the Chaco region. These fires are not only a result of climate change but also a man-made origins intended to expand the agricultural frontier. Therefore, a stricter control and emergency response to these forest fires will benefit the effectiveness of REDD-plus.

### 2.4 Needs of the recipient

Economic growth in Paraguay depends on attracting foreign direct investment as well as on exporting electricity from its agriculture sector (soybeans). Paraguay is the fifth largest soy producer in the world, and since 2014 it has experienced a significant increase in an average annual rate of 6 per cent due to strong production and high global prices [Central Intelligence Agency (CIA), 2019]. The country’s poverty rate is 35.8 per cent below the national poverty line. Much of the land is owned by a small number of individuals and successive governments have been unable to implement policies that would ensure tenure security for the poor.

Increased global demands for soya and beef could imply increased pressure on the forests. Breaking the cycle will require a national consensus, understanding the need for nature-based solutions in order to achieve sustainable development benefits.

The country has made efforts to implement the forestry law and has taken actions to reverse the deforestation trend in order to control deforestation in the Eastern Region. Action has also been taken towards strengthening institutional capacity to control deforestation. In this context, payments for environmental services (PES) have been implemented since 2014. This is part of that following the change in article 42 of the forestry law (by the previous government). Deforestation can be controlled by increasing the number of forest rangers and strengthening command and control options to ensure legal compliance with the laws, better information systems and strengthening the capacity of the Ministry of Environment, INFONA, the National Institute for Rural and Land Development, and other relevant institutions. The country is also applying GRIPs to the end users, with incentives and disincentives to stop deforestation.

Paraguay has a small portion of its population living in indigenous communities and smallholders to ensure better livelihoods. Even though they live in a small portion of the country and are not themselves driving deforestation, there could be options to allow reforestation on their lands as part of the national strategy for forest restoration.

Paraguay is dedicating just a small proportion of its budgetary expenditure to combat deforestation. Government spending in Paraguay averaged 2.43% (US$106 million) Paraguayan guaraní (PYG) (USD 387,562,393) from 1994 to 2019 (Trading Economics, 2019). The bulk of

## Country ownership

Paraguay has taken actions in REDD+ plus activities under the forestry-related opportunities created by the UNFCCC and the Warsaw Framework for REDD+. The nationally appropriate mitigation measures (NAMMs) are being developed at country level, with the ambition to reduce deforestation by 20 per cent based on business as usual projected.

Paraguay has taken a range of steps:

(a) The Government of Paraguay has developed national legislation to protect natural forests, prevent deforestation and restore forests in certain areas, within the Ministry of Environment. This includes the Forest Law (Law No. 422/73), which provides for environmental supervision, and INFORA in charge of applying the Forest Law 422/73. This is complemented by other laws such as the National Forest Code (Law No. 1058/96) with incentives for reforestation; designating forests as reserves for forest production or as some kind of protected area; and the Forest Management Plan (Law No. 422/73). Under Law 422/73, farms with forest areas are obliged to maintain 25 per cent of the original forest cover on their land. In addition, under the National Forest Code (Law No. 1058/96) -with a buffer zone of 100 metres on each side and where restoration of such forest takes place- this must be carried out with native species.

(b) The National Commission for Forests was consolidated through the Climate Change Law. The Commission is composed of permanent members and advisory members, including representatives of public institutions, private sector,

e). Paraguay has presented the payment for results report, accounting for 7% of the total volume of $820 million plus results between 2015 and 2017 (measured against FREL-B – the period of 2014–2015), equivalent to a total of 20,000,000 tCO2e. This includes a buffer of $8 per ton of CO2e for the period 2016 and 2017.

The United Nations Environment Programme’s accreditation only allows them to manage projects for a maximum period of five years. g). The effectiveness of the use of proceeds can only be evaluated in the future, when the impact of the projects is measured. These include the establishment of a national system of protected forests (ENMCS) and the scheme to provide incentives and disincentives through the climate change fund produce expected results in terms of changes in behaviors and avoided deforestation.

h). Paraguay proposes to use around 80 per cent of the resources to strengthen institutions, including the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development. The current budget proposes an important investment in monitoring and strengthening of operations with INFORF, INPAFCO and INPES, as proposed by the National Forest Law. These are welcome investments. However, they should go hand in hand with unequivocal political will to ensure that these resources are not used for other purposes or environmental duties. Otherwise, the investment will not be effective.

i). The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development together with INFORF could use the funds to strengthen its institutional capacity so that institutions are strengthened (consultations, staffing etc) and use the proceeds to encourage more permanent outcomes to avoid deforestation.

j). The USD 18.6 million assigned to the climate change fund is small amount to start an effective mechanism to incentivize good practices and discriminative agribusinesses that are driving deforestation. It is also important to note that the current system of payments for ecosystem services will need a constant flow of resources to ensure proper sustainability. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development proposes that blends finance from the private sector and public resources, ensuring sustainability of the fund. For example, it could be used to support the energy sector for the climate change fund in a more sustainable and permanent basis.

k). Following on the subject of incentives against deforestation and the full implementation of REDD+, Paraguay has presented a proposal to establish a system of high environmental certificates for projects considered as of high environmental impact. In designing the climate change fund, it is important that the criteria used are consistent with compliance status and pursuit was forward that are aligned with the REDD-plus strategy.

l). Paraguay has established a round table on sustainable finances to bridge the financial sector to the environment sector in order to develop innovative financing mechanisms and develop financial schemes that can effectively influence behaviour of the agribusinesses.

m). There is also an urgent need to start “defending deforestation”. In other words, Paraguay should establish a system of incentives against deforestation and all the incentives and disincentives in terms of land acquisition, land reform, fiscal regimes and financial schemes that support agrobusinesses without considering the consequences of deforestation.

n). As the world moves to prevent deforestation and encourage regenerative supply chains, the soyas and beef farmers of Paraguay will need to revise their models, and the country will

need to revise trade agreements, shifting to new methods in order to compete in the world markets.

52. As stated above, Paraguay has grown steadily in the last 10 years. There could be an increase in government expenditure to control deforestation and to support regeneration models. However, the current budgetary constraints, as well as the need for sustainable development and to INFONSA seem insufficient to ensure an effective REDD+ strategy.

### III. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

53. The Paraguay REDD+ project proposal presents a good case for financing under the GCF REDD pilot programme. However, because of this nature, the project proposal lacks sufficient information on the efficacy of the utilization of proceeds for the TAP to make specific and decisive remarks.

54. The ITAP recommends this project be approved by the Board.
- The ITAP recommends that Paraguay and the accredited entity give special attention to the following:
  (a) Continue efforts to improve the FREL, prioritizing:
    - Implementation of the proposed improvement plan in order to (i) address the inconsistencies between data used for the GCF inventory; (ii) improve the collection of information on activity data, emission factors and omitted carbon pools; and (iii) assess the significance of emissions of gases emitted from the FREL;
  (b) Estimate and report the aggregate FREL and uncertainties related to results in future years.
  (c) Finalize the studies allowing for the definition of forest degradation as well as methods and processes for generating activity data and emission factors, so that it may be possible to include this component in the FREL in relation to deforestation activity;
  (d) Continue capacity-building efforts to improve accuracy of the data and information collected on the FREL, including the development of guidelines regarding the consideration of post-deforestation carbon stocks and the methods to estimate activity data; and
  (e) Finalize the establishment of a system allowing registration and tracking of emission reductions from REDD+ pilot activities;
  (f) Given the accreditation scope of the accredited entity with a ceiling of USD 50 million, the amount of funding required for this project should be addressed in a future funding proposal for a subsequent Board meeting.

56. In terms of the use of the proceeds for the potential subsequent proposal, the long-term sustainable development and poverty reduction goals are consistent with those set out by the current project proposal. Paraguay proposed several lines of action toward this goal, including contributions from the private sector, public-private partnerships, and other innovative financing schemes. Lines of action confirmed with a plan around scalability, efficiency, capitalization and innovative blended finance prevention and incentive mechanisms, if Paraguay returns to GCF for additional REPPs.

57. Furthermore, the ITAP would welcome mechanisms and provisions to drive actions for “Subsidizing” deforestation-free agricultural production in order to create agroecological value chains that respond to the urgent need to stop deforestation and ensure regenerative agricultural production in Paraguay.

provided in its 2013 BUR because, for the purposes of the FREL activity data and emission factors have been updated owing to an institutional decision taken at the time of the submission of the BUR. The AT provided a detailed explanation and highlighted that consistency in terms of pools and gases is not required between the BUR and the TAR. Moreover, in paragraph 20 of the TAR, the AT specified that the information contained in the BUR (2013) and the forest inventory included in the BUR (2015) is not fully consistent with the FREL activity data and emission factors provided for particular for the year 2011 and in terms of carbon pools and gases reported in the BUR. The AT also noted that at the time of submission of the BUR only a preliminary version of the land use change (LUC) information was available, while the final LUC information from the national forest inventory was used for the baseline conversion factor calculation. At the time of the FREL submission, this information for two forest strata was not available as at least one year of data were required to calculate the emission factors provided by the IPCC good practice guidance for REDD+ activities (IPCC, 2013). Furthermore, Paraguay clarified that while in the BUR submission, the information contained in the BUR took into account the conversion of forest land to cropland and the conversion of forest land to cropland. With regard to the inclusion of carbon stock changes and intrasite (within) emissions, the AT noted that the difference between the FREL and Paraguay clarified that this difference was due to the notification of the national forest inventory as part of the submission of the BUR, based on the need to prioritize above-ground biomass. The AT also noted that the inclusion of intrasite emissions were considered to be more accurate considering the results from the national forest inventory. The AT concluded that the information provided among the documents should be an area of future improvement.
According to Paraguay's FREL TAR (paragraph 36) and in the


---
**Page 136**
---

FGB submission (all pages 4-8) the forest definition used in the construction of the FREL is the same as the one that the Party used in its national GCF inventory included as part of the 2013-2015 TAF. The forest definition used by Paraguay in this TAF does not explicitly contain or mention the forest definition used in the GCF submission (see paragraphs 74-77 of the TAF: http://biblioteca.seam.gov.py/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/TAF-PARAGUAY.pdf). It is also worth noting that the Forest Reference Emission Level (FREL) used in this TAF is not a single entity, but rather it is a composite of two different forest types and cover values. This means that the FREL contains two different definitions applicable to different regions of the country. Paraguay was only able to apply one of them (see paragraphs 72 and 73 of the TAF: http://biblioteca.seam.gov.py/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/TAF-PARAGUAY.pdf).

In response to the findings of the AT, Paraguay has proposed an improvement in the FREL construction. This includes the collection and analysis of data to complete the information on native forest cover values for different types of forests (e.g., humid tropical and soils). Moreover, the plan also considers the obtention of better data on primary forest conversion rates, deforestation, reforestation, and the improvement of capacities in implementing the IPCC 2013 Guidelines.

Paraguay is not a HFLD country. The construction of Paraguay's FREL was based on historical data and it is equal to or below the average annual historical emissions during the reference period, unless a country is an HFLD country.

Some issues were raised during the TAR which negatively affect the accuracy of the FREL/FRL:

• The AT noted that while the national forest inventory considers palm areas as part of the forest, they do not consider palm areas as an independent forest stratum. Paraguay's response was that palm trees are considered in assessing deforestation in the FREL; they are included in the land use change maps but they were included as part of the forest stratum (e.g., in the province of Alto Paraná and the Rio Paraguay) where most of the palm vegetation is located. The AT noted that this is based on available information, collected over three sample units, considered not reliable enough because it was

collected in a small area that does not fully represent the high variability of this forest type. In the modified submission, Paraguay has applied a weighted average by applying weights to the estimates derived independently for these two forest strata, which was recommended by the GCF in its letter dated 20 June 2017 (the “Letter”) on the basis of the sample size of the Sub Humedo Insandable del Río Paraguay” to derive the GCF’s estimate at the level of each forest stratum. The AT regarded this as an interim solution, but considered that it would be preferable to have independent forest stratum, in terms of activity data and estimation methodology, for the calculation of the estimates, avoiding the potential overestimation of certain forest strata due to technical limitations or technical improvements (paragraph 23 of the TAR). During the AT meeting, Paraguay explained that owing to the medium-resolution satellite images used and the lack of a detailed ground truth interpretation of the image, in practice only areas with a high biomass density (above 10 tons per hectare) can be unequivocally identified and interpreted. This was also the case when elaborating the 2011 forest cover map and the land use change maps. The AT considered that this analysis should be repeated at a higher forest definition in an area for future improvement (paragraph 24 of the TAR).

The AT noted that the allometric equations used to derive biomass estimates from diameter data were selected at the level of forest strata, while a large variability was observed within each stratum at the level of each forest stratum, leading therefore to a potentially overestimation of the total above-biomass stock (paragraph 24 of the TAR). Consequently, the AT recommended that the size of the sample size by the collection of diametric information on a larger number of sample units and

As regards the soil organic carbon in forest soils,
Paraguay explained that it had estimated the carbon stock for each of the forest strata and information on the methodology that was used to estimate this carbon stock. The preliminary results cannot be used for the estimation of CO2 emissions from deforestation, because they do not include the carbon content of soil for other land-use categories, which is needed for the calculation of the net change in carbon stocks due to deforestation.
Furthermore, Paraguay clarified in the modified submission that the forest soils are converted into cropland and other lands, as included in its GEF inventory. The conversion of these soils from forest to soil is oxidized after the conversion. The AT agreed that the lack of information on the amount of carbon stock lost due to soil oxidation after the conversion prevents a proper estimation of the emissions, as the assumption that all the carbon stock would be oxidized would overestimate the emissions from this pool.
The AT therefore considered that the data provided at the moment, adequately justified by Paraguay; but, noting the magnitude of the uncertainties involved in the data provided by Paraguay, considered the assessment of the significance of these soils as a significant source an area for further improvement (paragraph 31 of the TAR).
Paraguay explained that the carbon stock from soil organic carbon in organic soils was excluded from the proposed FREL owing to the lack of scientific knowledge and data on this subject. The AT agreed with Paraguay, and during the exchange of information that some studies could be launched for the assessment of the carbon stock content of these soils within the country.
The AT considered that, depending upon how much deforestation takes place in these areas where deforestation takes place, this pool might be a significant source of GHG emissions. Given the uncertainty of carbon inventories in these soils, therefore, the assessment of the significance of these soils as a significant source is an area for further improvement (paragraph 31 of the TAR).

The LULUCF experts noted that the data and methods provided in the technical annex to be transparent, consistent, comparable, and complete (see paragraphs 31 and 47 of the TATF). The LULUCF expert assessment results are accurate for the periods based on the assumption accepted. The LULUCF experts found that Paraguay is taking steps to use updated information from the most recent VIT inventory in its next LULUCF expert assessment and is considering including additional activities and land uses in future assessments (see paragraph 49 of the TATF).

### (v) Is the data and information provided in the technical annex accurate? (Does it neither over- nor underestimate emissions and/or removals?)

Some of the most relevant issues pertaining to the accuracy of the technical annex that the LULUCF experts found were that:
* Both the gross emissions from deforestation for 2015–2017 from the implementation of the activity reduction measure and the assumption that deforestation takes place in areas of intact forest land use are not taken into account. Accordingly, the gross emissions from deforestation were estimated for 2015–


---
**Page 145**
---

2017 following the same approach as used for the FREE.

The LULUCF experts noted that if the carbon densities in these areas had been reduced (e.g., due to forest fires) or increased (e.g., due to afforestation or reforestation) before a deforestation event, then emissions from deforestation and degradation would have been underestimated. In response, Paragraph explained that enhancements of carbon stocks were included in the rank used to track deforestation. Paragraph also noted that the GCF/24/INF/016 approach (degradation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks) is not fully consistent with the FTFR.

The LULUCF experts also noted that not considering the urbanization process in the assessment could lead to an overestimation of the emissions from deforestation and degradation. Paragraph explained this intention to include removals from the land following deforestation in the GCF/24/INF/016 approach (paragraph 40 of the FTFR).

Two forest inventories were developed for Paraguay: one for 2015-2016, and another for 2016-2017. These were developed by the National Forest Institute (INIFOR) using remote sensing data to provide information on forest cover changes. For 2015-2016, a number of images were included in the assessment, while for 2016-2017 only one image was used.

LULUCF experts asked Paraguay whether the increase in deforestation and degradation between 2014 and 2015 is due to the difference in the window for image processing. In response, Paragraph explained that a significant part of the deforestation in one year was included in the following year's assessment (i.e., 2016). Therefore, the difference does not have a negative impact on the overall results.

There were various reasons why images from different years had to be used: quality of images, contractual or operational constraints. In total, more than 10 per cent of images were affected by this problem.


---
**Page 146**
---

The images cover the period July 2013 to June-July 2017. The LULUCF experts noted that, in order to maintain consistency over time, Paraguay could consider maintaining the same methodology and data sources for ensuing for subsequent results assessments (paragraphs 19 and 41 of the Technical Note). The LULUCF experts also noted that Paraguay further noted that is in the process to complete the PRL period evaluation for the year 2015, which will be evaluated for thematic accuracy. The period analysis is approximately 3 years (July 2013 – June-July 2016) and subsequent years, and that it is now starting to work with Sentinel-1 data to improve the quality of the images and increase the probability of good quality and low cloud presence in the images. This will allow for the inclusion of additional staff, capacity building and validation of methodologies. The PRL period evaluation was at a resolution of 20 meters per pixel compared to 30 meters in Landcover 1.0. The LULUCF experts noted that deforestation every six months as required by the National Forest Inventory (NFI) will be included into the ongoing process that spans each 12-month period.

As noted in the FP (section B.2.17 (c)), the Government of Paraguay has not received any payment for the period from 2015 to 2017. In addition, no request has been made to other potential buyers or sellers of REDD+ credits for payments related to processes for periods before or after 2015-2017. Likewise, the Government of Paraguay has not received any payment of a kind that commits REDD+ emissions reductions achieved by the country.

In Paraguay, there are two projects developed for the voluntary carbon market (VCM) and one project under the voluntary reduction of deforestation and forest degradation. These projects have international reference levels (IRLs) according to the CDM Standard. Estimates of annual removals (removals or reductions in emissions) from these projects during 2015-2017 are equivalent to 89% of the 26.79 million tons of CO2e of the total volume offered by Paraguay to the GCF for inclusion in the period 2015–2017; the reductions or emissions correspond to 141% of this volume.

The Government of Paraguay has not received any compensation because they have already benefited from a private sector project that is included in the registry. There are no potential removals or emission reductions of these projects with the intention of being registered and sold on the Green Climate Fund until now for any type of current or future volume while, at the same time, the national registry mentioned above.

According to the FP, the Government of Paraguay, through MAHES and the National Commission on Climate Change, is in the process of developing a registry for mitigation actions. The registry will include mitigation actions whether national or regional in nature. This registry could be implemented either nationally or regionally; however, it would be more effective to implement the registry locally. Starting with this

# Response to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP 121)

## Proposal name: REDD+ Results-based payments in Paraguay for the period 2015-2017

## Accredited entity: United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

### Impact potential

Paraguay takes note of the comments and recommendations. The country is currently refining the structure of the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF). The actions selected from the NCCF are designed to support the implementation of the REDD+ strategy, including investments in enforcement and monitoring capacities, investments in response/alert capacities, including fire response, and investments in incentives [Law 3081/06], income diversification, and capacity building. These investments will contribute to the financial sustainability of the NCCF. Finally, based on 2017 data reported in the BUR, the area of remaining forest cover in Paraguay is 42%. This includes large tracts of continuous forests.

### Paradigm shift potential

Paraguay fully agrees on that success in the implementation of Law 3081/06 requires enforcement of the forestry law, which in turn requires strong political support. The proceeds from results-based payments (RBPs) will be used to improve enforcement capacities and political commitment. The proposal also includes a major investment in response/alert capacities, including fire response, as the TAP recommends.

### Sustainable development potential

Paraguay takes note of comments and recommendations. A key strategy is to increase capacities to enforce the forestry law in medium and large farms while in parallel strengthening incentives to protect forest cover (Law 3081/06). The proposal also includes, as recommended by the TAP, a major investment in response/alert capacities for small landholders and communities. We also agree with the importance of understanding the current situation regarding the implementation of the NCCF and the progress in Paraguay, to which RBPs will also contribute. Finally, the proposal includes a major investment in fire response capacities, as the TAP recommends.

### Needs of the recipient

Paraguay takes note of the suggestions from the TAP to apply innovative financial mechanisms for REDD+ results-based payments investments to combat deforestation, including contributions from the energy sector and blended finance. The NCCF has a window intended to tap private sector financing in order to increase its investment portfolio and improve access to RBPs. Paraguay also acknowledges the need to increase its own investments into deforestation activities. Finally, while the country is not growing in the past years, income


---
**Page 152**
---

basic needs are still high. Improving human development will require substantial and steady public investment in social areas.

### Country ownership

Paragraph 106 acknowledges the recommendation from the ITAP. There has been increasing involvement of the REDD+ process by stakeholders and SRPs will help to keep and increase participation. We take note that the current administration reversed the Decree that had allowed for a public consultation on the draft National Strategy (the feedback received from the public was important in driving this decision). We also acknowledge that it will be necessary to complement the efforts of the government with those of civil society, local communities, and public-private coalitions for anti-deforestation actions. The proposed use of RFPs, particularly through the NGCT, includes actions specifically targeted towards these objectives.

### Efficient and Effective Implementation

Paraguay would like to assure the ITAP that SRPs will not be used to pay for MAIIS and/or INFINOA's staff, nor they will support unnecessary studies and consultancy. These will be kept to the bare minimum in order to maximize investments on capacities for enforcement monitoring and management of forest resources. The same applies for the NGCT. It is also due note of the need to increase budgetary allocations to control deforestation and support forest restoration activities, as well as to cover recurrent costs for INFINOA and MAIIS to complement HIPS and make an efficient use of them.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Paraguay would like to thank the ITAP for the constructive review. Both Paraguay and the AE commit themselves to take the necessary actions to address the comments and recommendations as set in the Overall Recommendations. In particular, it is important to highlight that the description of eligibility and areas of priority of the NGCT, as shown in Annex 6, provides assurances to the GCF and AE that the NGCT will be implemented in a manner consistent with and compatible with the GCF and AE policies. The NGCT also includes a dedicated window to leverage private sector investments for forest conservation and sustainable land management. Paraguay will continue to work with ongoing local initiatives to involve agriculture in deforestation-free common lands, as well as with the implementation of the National Forest Strategy. The results of FRELS and the reporting of results are genuinely welcome. Paraguay has already started to work on some of them and plans to address them all in the short and medium term.

# 1. Introduction

Understanding the specific situation of women and men is an important prerequisite for addressing gender inequality, as it forms a basis for generating appropriate and comprehensive responses. It is essential to integrate the availability of gender-disaggregated data and analysis of gender-related information to guide the design of programmes and projects. This document provides a brief overview of the current situation in Paraguay with regard to the gap between the situation of women and men, are describe their characteristics, measure their magnitude and identify key challenges.

In the development of the current proposal "Paraguay REDD+ RBP for results period 2015-2017", UN Environment assessed potential risks, benefits, impacts and risks for women and men. The results of this assessment will be further refined at the beginning of the project and throughout its implementation. Based on the assessment, the project will include actions to avoid, minimize, and/or mitigate any adverse gender impacts or risks. These actions are intended to ensure that the project contributes to the empowerment and development of women in the forestry and land-use sector. An initial action plan setting out what is to be done and by whom, when, where and how will be developed at the inception stage and reviewed at key milestones throughout the life time of the project (i.e., inception stage, annual review, mid-term evaluation).

Particular attention was paid to the different challenges faced by women and girls within indigenous communities. The project will aim to ensure that women and men are involved in all stages of the project, including the participation and leadership of women in all activities. There are six elements that will be considered for gender mainstreaming throughout the life time of this project: these are:

(i) strengthening accountability for gender equity at different levels;
(ii) enhancing results-based management for gender equity using indicators and protocols for evaluation;
(iii) establishing oversight through monitoring, evaluation, auditing and reporting;
(iv) disseminating information on gender equality;
(v) capacity building for gender mainstreaming; and
(vi) ensuring the availability of knowledge and data on gender.

The main needs and priorities of women and men that were identified through stakeholder engagement activities undertaken in the preparation of the project include those related to: access to natural resources, including forest resources, sustainable livelihoods and income-generating activities; formalization or land tenure; education and economic opportunities; and benefit sharing.

A gender specialist will be hired for the Project Management Unit of the programme and provided with a comprehensive mandate, which will ensure gender mainstreaming throughout the design and implementation of project activities. UN Environment will undertake further gender assessments at the activity level and at the project level as well as at the project level according to identified needs and to UN Environment and GCF guidance on gender.

# 2. Existing gender dynamics and inequalities in Paraguay

In Paraguay, women and men experience different socio-economic conditions. The less favourable situation of women is related to social-cultural factors linked to the division of labour, which affect the


---
**Page 156**
---

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Document associated with the Proposal for REDD+ Results-Based Payments in Paraguay for the Period 2015-2017

## possibility of access to education, training and technical advice, credit and land ownership: Lower education levels and limited access to specific training and information on land rights, credit and technology hinder economic empowerment and possibilities to generate income and make decisions about expenditures and resources. Independent women have more opportunities for decision-making power than men over land use and less access to land. Cultural barriers have an enormous impact and are often difficult to overcome. Customs, traditions, beliefs and stereotypes still hamper the exercise of rights by women in rural areas.

## Evidence of these unequal circumstances is provided by the statistics presented below.

### a. Gender Equality Index

The Gender Equality Index is a tool that measures how far or close countries are from achieving a gender equal society, where 100 stands for the best possible situation. The Gender Equality Index measures gender inequalities in three important aspects of human development: health, empowerment and economic status.

| Gender Equality Index Paraguay | 69.4 (Ranking 55 out of 129 countries) (2019)* |
|---|---|
| **Education** |

With respect to gender gaps in education in Paraguay, the country has made progress in recent years, nevertheless there is still limited access of girls and women to higher education, especially in rural areas.

| Literacy Rates | 94.89% of Men and 80.18% of Women (2017)* |
| Primary school attendance | 95.20% of Boys and 94.37% of Girls (2017)* |

### c. Labour force participation rate and unemployment rate

Women in Paraguay, particularly in rural areas, have lower economic revenues than men. Women's access to the labour force is limited and strongly determined by social and family circumstances. Due to domestic and care responsibilities, women are less likely to participate in paid work outside their homes or to be involved in participating in the formal labour force, and in terms of receiving regular salaries, social security, work stability and union protection. Economic growth in Paraguay has contributed little towards reducing the economic gender gap.

## d. Political participation rate

Women's participation in politics and decision-making processes in Paraguay have improved in recent years, but still reflect significant inequalities. There has been a slow increase in the number of women in elected positions. The Electoral Code establishes quotas for women, but these are only valid for internal selection within political parties. These quotas do not necessarily have an impact on general election results. In the high-level political positions, women hold very low representation. This reflects the difficulties that women will face to have actively participate in decision-making.

## e. Health and sexual self-determination

Maternal mortality is among the highest in the region, and Paraguay also has high rates of teenage pregnancy. The physical autonomy of women is affected by violence both inside and outside of the family, as well as by limited choice in terms of sexuality and reproduction.

| f. Access to land |

In Paraguay, access to land is still a factor that determines the livelihoods of most of the rural population. Current land tenure is characterized by a high concentration of land in the hands of a small group of landowners and by the lack of formal titles for women.

Women suffer even more from land tenure inequality than men. Between 1940 and 2008, women received 13.6% of fiscal land allocated to peasant families. They have also been marginalized in access to productive inputs and credit.

According to a UN Women publication on land and gender in Paraguay[1], "increased control of land by women could contribute to the survival of peasant and indigenous communities in the country, to sustainable development, and to the conservation of natural resources and biodiversity. It would also contribute to the mitigation and adaptation of climate change. It is not only necessary to transform the current land tenure structure, but also to invest in improving the living conditions of the rural population and, above all, in promoting gender equality."

There is widespread consensus on the need to eliminate the gender inequality reflected in the figures above, in order to ensure equal access to productive resources such as land, water, seeds, tools, and other resources. To this end, the project aims to ensure a comprehensive approach that fully and equally incorporates women's perspectives and contributions (see sections 4, 5 and 6 below).

## Legal and administrative framework protecting women and promoting gender equality

### International protocols and frameworks ratified by Paraguay

Paraguay is signatory of several international conventions that focus on gender equality and women's empowerment[2].

* The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), adopted in 1979 by the UN General Assembly, often described as a "bill of rights" for women. Consisting of a preamble and 30 articles, it defines what constitutes discrimination against women and sets out an agenda for national action to end such discrimination.[^1] The convention was ratified by Paraguay in 1987.
* International Pact on Civil and Political Rights (referred to in Law No. 5/92), and its optional protocols.
* International Pact on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (Law No. 492).
* Inter-American Convention against All Forms of Discrimination and Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women (Belem do Pará) (Law No. 605/95).
* International Labour Organisation (ILO) Convention No. 156 concerning Equality of Opportunity and Treatment Between Men and Women Workers, Women with Family Responsibilities (Law No. 338/07).

## b. National frameworks, policies and laws

There is recognition in the policy and legal framework of Paraguay of the above-mentioned realities, and efforts have been made to promote gender equality through, for example, the following frameworks, policies and laws.

The National Constitution of 1993 guarantees equality of rights and non-discrimination, explicitly establishing equality of persons, equality of rights between women and men and the requirement of non-discrimination, providing that the State must promote the conditions and create adequate mechanisms for such respect. The Constitution also prohibits any act or practice that impede or hinder its exercise and facilitating the participation of women in all spheres of national life.

* Art 46: Equality of people.
* Art 47: Equality between men and equality
* Art 48: Equal rights of men and women
* Art 49: Elimination of violence against women
* Art 88: Non-discrimination
* Art 89: Women's work

Furthermore, Paraguay created a Women’s Secretariat in 1992, which became the Women’s Ministry in 2012 (Law No. 547/12) and then the Secretary of Women to the Ministry of Women). It also developed a National Plan for Equal Opportunities between Women and Men for the period 2008-2017[^1], in addition to other policies and laws.

Additionally, Paraguay also relies on the following legal instruments that consider gender aspects:

* Law 577/16 on comprehensive protection for women against all forms of violence.
* Law 568/15 on promotion and protection of motherhood and support for breastfeeding.
* Law 492/03 on the elimination of discrimination in employment, with a view to promote and guarantee the economic, social, political and cultural rights of rural women that are fundamental for their empowerment and development.

In November 2016, the National Government, through the Ministry of Women, organized the first of a series of planned working meetings among the units in charge of gender issues across the Executive Authority.[^8]

## 3. Gender, forests and REDD+ in Paraguay

According to FAO, approximately 1.2 billion people around the world depend on agroforestry and forest resources for their livelihoods. At least 50% of these are women, which corresponds to a total of around 600 million women living in and depending on forests. However, the forest sector generally still lacks gender equality in its policies and practices. In order to ensure that the benefits from REDD+ and other initiatives in the management of forests, we need to be aware that men and women often have different roles and needs and a differentiated approach is required. This is particularly true when it comes to the impacts of previously unknown or ignored by interventions related to forest management, which led either to failed initiatives or to increases in gender inequalities within the communities. The gender approach in forestry starts with a better understanding of how men and women use forests on the ground.

Economic, social, cultural, political and legal environments affect the right and ability of women and men to control forest resources and own land. Access to land alone does not guarantee access to forest products and opportunities for income generation.

In many countries, women are the primary users of certain forest resources and therefore they play an essential role in forest management, conservation and forest-based climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies. Their rights on forests and forests products are more at stake for women when forests are degraded or disappear.

In Paraguay, there are three challenges to address: gender gaps between men and women in relation to economic opportunities; the lack of land tenure security for rural areas within urban areas; and in areas with high forest cover and high poverty rates, there tends to be a relatively high dependence of communities on forests for their livelihoods, especially in adverse economic situations. Implementation of REDD+ policies and measures in Paraguay should therefore take into account these challenges from a gender approach, can benefit local livelihoods by helping to clarify and strengthen land tenure rights, increasing community participation in forest management, and by mainstreaming ecosystem services important for both food security and climate change adaptation.

The design and implementation of REDD+ policies and measures in Paraguay are based on a gender mainstreaming approach that takes into account the range of gender roles (active, passive, reactive), and specific challenges of different projects and programmes included in the UN-REDD Programme National Programme.

In the final evaluation[1] of the UN-REDD National Programme in Paraguay, challenges related to gender, and vulnerabilities of women, were highlighted, including the fact that in Paraguay, 1% of the population owns 80% of the land, and women are owners of just 27% of private land (see also section 2.1 above). There was also an observation that climate finance, when merged with private sector investment, tends to

## a. REDD+ Gender Approach

The UN-REDD Programme's gender approach pursues three complementary objectives, as follows:

* Ensure that the priorities of gender equality and the empowerment of women inform the formulation, preparation and implementation of REDD+ policies and measures so they are fully integrated into them.
* Comply with provisions on gender equality contained in international agreements on REDD+, including safeguards; and
* Promote gender equality in planning processes, implementation, monitoring and knowledge management of REDD+ in order to apply REDD+ in a manner that promotes a gender perspective and contributes to SDG 5 on gender equality.

The five pillars of the UN-REDD Programme's gender methodology as listed (a detailed description can be found in the document "UN-REDD Gender Guidelines, 2017") are:

I. Gender-sensitive evaluations and gender-specific analyses
II. Gender awareness and capacity-building
III. Participation and empowerment
IV. Planning and monitoring with a gender perspective
V. Gender mainstreaming

These orientations have guided much of the work on embedding gender considerations within Paraguay's activities on REDD+ in recent years and will continue to be relevant as the country begins to scale up implementation.

## b. Gender considerations in the "Estrategia Nacional de Bosques para el Crecimiento Sostenible" (ENBSC)

The ENBSC has been constructed collectively, with a gender approach and the promotion of participation of key actors from civil society organizations, indigenous peoples, women's groups, men and women involved and involved in the REDD+ work following the guidelines of the stakeholder identification and mapping carried out as part of the implementation of the National Programme and the "Stakeholder Identification and Mapping Platform" (PIS) developed by the ENBSC. The process of development, realization and dissemination of the ENBSC, and the policies and measures it establishes, was carried out with representatives of the public sector, private sector, civil society organizations, indigenous peoples, women's groups, men and women involved and financial entities, with a cross-cutting gender approach and continued engagement and participation of women's groups. The process of development and dissemination of the ENBSC was coordinated through the REDD+ Working Group, which was created and formed within the framework of the National Commission on Climate Change, with the aim of becoming a space for discussion and technical consultations on issues related to REDD+.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

The ENBGS has six strategic and three cross-cutting measures, under which several actions include specific gender approaches and others focus on vulnerable populations in general, including women. The ENBGS aims to reduce gender gaps specifically by:

* Ensuring the participation of women in all spheres of decision making related to forests, considering the differentiated roles related to forests and dependency on forest resources between men and women.
* And by aiming to ensure equal access to services like forest-related education, credit and also to land.

Specific actions in the ENBGS which can help address vulnerability in general include:

| Diversify income sources and sustainable production systems, including through the use of multiple benefits from forests. |
| --- |
| Facilitate access for local communities and women's groups to formal credit systems for the adoption of forest-based production technologies that contribute to emissions reductions; |
| Strengthen the market for differentiated products from forests; |
| Promote the participation of women in decision-making processes in indigenous communities, focused on the domestic use of firewood from the native forest, and highlighting the role of women. |
| Develop and incentivize production processes through the implementation of agroforestry, diversified livestock systems and other sustainable practices. |
| Prioritize payment for environmental services in indigenous communities. |

To achieve gender equality, the ENBGS seeks to reduce the economic gap between men and women in an integral and systematic way. The ENBGS aims to reduce gender gaps by ensuring the participation of women in all spheres of decision making related to forests, considering the differentiated roles related to forests and dependency on forest resources from men and women; and by aiming to ensure equal access to services like forest-related education, credit and also to land.

The implementation of the ENBGS will contribute to reducing gender gaps in the environmental and rural sectors more widely, and contributes to meeting the objectives outlined in the National Gender and Climate Change Strategy (ENGCC, in Spanish). This Strategy, which was published in 2017, constitutes an efficient framework for the coordination between the Ministry of Environment and the Ministry of Climate change initiatives, so that men and women can live with dignity and use natural resources in a sustainable way. Its four pillars are:

(1) Institutional capacity building;

(2) adequate financing;

(3) gender equality promotion and citizen participation;

(4) knowledge management and technology.

The guidelines established in the ENBGS are linked to the principles established in the ENGCC.

The ENGCC recognizes existing gender gaps in the country, and links a gender approach with elements of the legal framework related to climate change, focusing on Paraguay's National Policy on Climate Change (PNPC). The PNPC establishes a set of guidelines for the formulation of policies and actions to tackle climate change, by promoting equal opportunities and engagement of women and men in the achievement of sustainable development targets. The Strategy establishes a national

## c. Gender considerations for the project "Paraguay REDD+" RBP for results period 2015-2017"

Gender sensitive and gender responsive approaches have been incorporated in the modalities of the project. Thus, gender considerations have guided the definition of activities planned for the use of proceeds, and will be taken into account throughout the implementation phase.

The specific circumstances, roles and gaps under the project have the potential to support greater equality and empowerment of women in relation to control over and access to forests and carbon resources, replicated with the following provisions:

- Land-use planning
- Forest management planning and management planning for protected areas;
- Land titling
- Support to sustainable production models, including through access to training, credit and other services.
- Planning for Environmental Services; and
- Control and enforcement of forest and environmental legislation.

At the same time, the project will ensure equal opportunities for women and men in employment created by the programme. The specific circumstances, roles and gaps under the project have been considered at the local level before and during the implementation phase, taking into account the differentiated roles related to forests and dependency over forest resources between men and women; and is ensured equal opportunities for women and men in employment created by the programme.

In addition, the specific circumstances, gender roles and gaps will be considered at the local level before and during the implementation phase. The project will ensure equal opportunities for women and men farmers (campesino) communities and small farmers (campesinos) communities and as part of the monitoring and evaluation process.

As mentioned previously, the ENBCIS, which will be supported under Component 1 of the project, aims to ensure equal opportunities for women and men in employment created by the programme. The specific circumstances, gender roles and gaps related to forests and dependency over forest resources between men and women; and is ensured equal opportunities for women and men farmers (campesino) communities and small farmers (campesinos) communities as part of the monitoring and evaluation process.

The project will ensure equal opportunities for women and men in employment created by the programme, including through access to training, credit and other services. The project will also improve the participation of women and their access to services related to forest management and the management of the Climate Change Fund. Altogether, the project will generate equal opportunities for women and men; seeking to reduce existing gaps in the country within the scope of action of the project.

The gender approach will also be integrated into the procedures for operation, responsibilities, accountability, structuring and the criteria for allocation of resources to the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF) and the ENBCIS. The specific circumstances, roles and gaps under the project have been considered at the local level before and during the implementation phase.

The project will ensure equal opportunities for women and men in employment created by the programme, including through access to training, credit and other services.
